# CERTIFY OR REFUSE: A CROSS-MODEL MAP FOR SELECTIVE RISK CONTROL WITH COVERAGE FLOORS UNDER COVARIATE SHIFT

A PREPRINT

Jiamiao Liu Dewen Qiao Yu Zhang Xuetao Chen\* Department of Information, Xinqiao Hospital   
Army Medical University (Third Military Medical University) Chongqing 400037, China xuetao@tmmu.edu.cn

## ABSTRACT

Selective predictors with certified risk attain whatever coverage they attain; operators, however, impose an automation floor: answer at least a β-fraction of shifted target traffic while keeping at most an α-fraction of returned answers wrong. Under bounded-ratio covariate shift, we prove the Floor Certification Map: once that hard coverage floor β must be certified alongside the selectionconditioned risk level α, certification acquires a feasibility frontier and a two-resource samplecomplexity map, additive up to constants: risk paid in labeled source samples, the floor in unlabeled target samples. Bounded-ratio shift alone buys the frontier; the two-resource rates are local, holding under a regular frontier margin and at slack below the local-regime threshold, with the upper bounds on a pre-registered nested-threshold lattice meeting a lattice margin condition and the lower bound on a compatible lattice constructed per slack. The displayed resource split is the operational route, since under oracle weights the floor additionally admits a labeled-source estimate that only weakens the requirement. We give the map as three model-tagged results: a lower bound (Model-B), a matching oracle-weight upper bound (Model-A), and an implementable upper bound (Model-B<sup>′</sup>), valid under a pre-registered exact stratified-shift model with nuisance cost priced explicitly. The match is across these three models rather than a single-model minimax theorem, and necessarily so: over the full bounded-ratio class no unknown-weight procedure matches at any sample size (Model-B is inconsistent, witnessed at $\begin{array} { r } { \alpha = \beta = \frac { 1 } { 2 } ) } \end{array}$ . So no single-model law holds over that class, and the cross-model map is the correct object, not a substitute for one. The nuisance’s necessity is only partially settled. The upper bound’s variance term is an accepted-region quantity bounded by a localized second moment, and the lower-bound hard slices localize to the same region, with no global effective-sample-size (ESS) term appearing on either side, though a fixed-ESS separation theorem is left open; both lower-bound axes vanish as $\beta  0 ,$ so the floor creates the map. Empirically, the registered bite family diverges with log–log slope −2.002 (within its pre-registered band); a 1,024-cell audit records 0 violations where the formal certificates fire; and a single-corpus SQuAD→NewsQA feasibility audit returns honest refusal.

Keywords: selective risk control, covariate shift, conformal risk control, distribution-free certification, sample complexity lower bounds

## 1 Introduction

An operator deploying a selective question-answering system commits to answering automatically at least a β-fraction of target traffic, with at most an α-fraction of the automatically returned answers wrong. Given n labeled samples from the calibration distribution and m unlabeled queries from the shifted target, when can this double promise be certified, and when is the only honest output a refusal?

(Audit protocol and per-arm Clopper–Pearson bounds: Table 4.)

Selection-conditioned risk control exists in several forms (linear-expectation reformulation [Wang et al., 2025b], weighted e-values [Bai and Jin, 2026], cascaded calibration [Jia et al., 2026]), built on selective classification [Geifman and El-Yaniv, 2017], risk control as multiple testing [Angelopoulos et al., 2021], and weighted conformal prediction [Tibshirani et al., 2019]; heuristic confidence deferral provably degrades under shift [Jitkrittum et al., 2023]. Yet all of these certify the risk of whatever coverage they attain. The classical reject-option line does characterize a risk–coverage frontier, but only the population frontier under a guaranteed-coverage (bounded-abstention) objective [Franc et al., 2023]. What is missing is the finite-sample object: no prior work proves lower bounds indexed by a coverage floor under covariate shift, nor maps what that floor costs in labeled versus unlabeled data; the nearest distribution-free lower-bound theory [Aldirawi et al., 2026] is floor-free: one axis, no coverage-floor parameter anywhere. Concretely, a risk-only certificate can be vacuously safe by abstaining on nearly all queries; it bounds the error of the few answers it does return while never guaranteeing that the operator’s β-fraction of target traffic is automated at all; the floor is exactly the promise these certificates cannot make. Figure 1 previews both halves of the result: the map’s two-resource certifiable region in the $( n , m )$ plane (left), and the full-grid validity audit where the formal certificates fire with zero violations (right).

This paper shows the floor changes the statistical problem. We prove the Floor Certification Map: a hard coverage floor under covariate shift splits certification cost into a labeled-source risk axis and an unlabeled-target floor axis. We also run a single-corpus SQuAD→NewsQA feasibility audit of the certificate’s honest refusal. Certifying means selecting one policy λ from a pre-fixed lattice of thresholds and returning the certificate $\ ^ { \mathfrak { s } } R _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \leq \alpha$ and $\bar { C } _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \ge \beta$ with confidence $1 - \delta _ { \circ } ^ { \prime \prime }$ or else issuing an honest refusal ⊥ when no policy can be certified. The frontier $\beta ^ { * } ( \alpha , Q )$ is the largest coverage certifiable at risk α.

Three models tag how much is known about the covariate-shift weights w: Model-A (oracle weights, w given exactly: the rate reference), Model-B (unknown weights, only $w \leq B$ known: the lower-bound model), and Model-B<sup>′</sup> (weights estimated under a pre-registered exact stratified-shift model, nuisance priced explicitly). The map states three modeltagged bounds: a Model-B lower bound, a Model-A oracle upper bound attaining the same rates, and an implementable Model-B<sup>′</sup> upper bound.

At feasibility-frontier slack s, a canonical rate template organizes all three bounds:

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } { \underbrace { s ^ { \mathrm { c e r t i f i a b l e } } } _ { \mathrm { s l a c k t h r e s h o l d } } } & { \asymp } & { \underbrace { \kappa ^ { - 1 } \sqrt { \beta / n } } _ { \mathrm { l a b e l e d : \ n s k } } + \underbrace { \sqrt { \beta / m } } _ { \mathrm { u n l a b e l e d : \ f l o o r } } + \underbrace { \rho / \kappa } _ { \mathrm { s h i f t - m o d e l \ n u i s a n c e } } . } \end{array}\tag{1}
$$

Here κ is the local frontier margin and $\rho$ the accepted-region shift-model nuisance, both made precise in $\ S 3 .$ . The display is an organizing template, not a same-model law: each model-tagged claim refines it.

Scope of the map. The displayed axes hold at a fixed bounded-ratio constant $B ,$ whose factors are folded into the Model-A constants and appear explicitly in the Model-B<sup>′</sup> nuisance axis $( B ^ { 2 } K$ , where K is the number of pre-registered shift cells; Claim 3). Theorem 8 shows this axis is the histogram estimator’s price, not a proved-necessary axis. The necessary core is K-free (lattice-valued), leaving only the unknown-η edge open. The displayed split (risk in labeled source, floor in unlabeled target) is the operational Model-B/B<sup>′</sup> route; under oracle weights (Model-A) the floor additionally admits a source-weighted (pooled) estimate, which only weakens the requirement (Appendix C).

Two structural facts shape the map. First, the lower bounds are Le Cam pairs at bounded likelihood ratio, so the impossibility vanishes as $\beta  0 \colon$ the map is floor-created, not overlap-created. Second, the upper-bound variance proxy is driven by a localized accepted-region second moment $\mathbb { E } _ { P } [ w ^ { 2 } S _ { \lambda } ]$ , not by global effective sample size (ESS); its normalized reading $\frac { \mathbb { E } _ { P } \left[ w ^ { 2 } S _ { \lambda } \right] } { ( \mathbb { E } _ { P } \left[ w S _ { \lambda } \right] ) ^ { 2 } }$ is heuristic (Remark 11). The lower-bound hard-slice geometry localizes to the same accepted region, so the correspondence runs through the matched bound structure rather than a standalone argument. A fixed-ESS separation theorem is left open.

Contributions (what the floor changes; how the cost splits across resources; what functional governs hardness; what the certificate honestly does on real shift).

• (1) The Floor Certification Map. Theorem 1 proves the two-axis map as three model-tagged bounds, with the Model-B<sup>′</sup> nuisance budget explicitly priced $( \rho _ { \lambda } \lesssim \kappa s )$ . The bounds match across models, and a single-model match over the full bounded-ratio class is provably impossible (Model-B inconsistency, Theorem 6). A within- ${ \bf - B ^ { \prime } }$ refinement sharpens the two resource axes (Corollary 2), and a necessary K-free core for the nuisance is established (Theorem 8) [§4].

• (2) The geometry. Experiments identify the localized accepted-region functional $\frac { \mathbb { E } _ { P } \left[ w ^ { 2 } S _ { \lambda } \right] } { ( \mathbb { E } _ { P } \left[ w S _ { \lambda } \right] ) ^ { 2 } }$ , not global ESS, as the operative complexity proxy for $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ required-n (family-1 Spearman .936 vs. .026; the restriction is part of the claim) [§5.1].

• (3) Mechanism and validity at scale. In the synthetic study, the Registered-family reproduces the map’s central prediction: the labeled-sample requirement blows up near the certifiable boundary at a near-quadratic rate (the “bite” divergence, log–log slope −2.002 inside the pre-registered exponent band; near-quadratic across three structurally distinct families). A full-grid validity audit records 0 violations and 0 Holm rejections where the formal certificate arms fire, against 108/40/662 for the non-formal demonstration arms (floor-free/weightedconformal/plug-in), and characterizes the certification envelope including $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime \prime } \mathbf { \hat { s } }$ K-premium [§5.1].

• (4) Real-workload feasibility audit (honest refusal). On a real-workload SQuAD→NewsQA audit, the certificate returns honest refusal-with-attribution, consuming no evaluation labels, and a post-hoc diagnosis places the estimated frontier below the operational floor across the tested plane [§5.3].

Full proofs and extended experimental records are in Appendices A–H.

## 2 Related Work and Theorem-Level Comparison

We delimit our claim sharply against the strongest neighbors. What is new is the covariate-shift theory the floor creates, the β-indexed lower bounds, the two-resource map, the localized complexity, and the budgeted-nuisance analysis, none of which these lines produce: no lower bound indexed by a coverage floor, no β-indexed matching rates, no frontier and feasibility theory. The certificate’s validity half is not new, and we say so concretely: a floor-augmented e-value construction in the spirit of SCoRE [Bai and Jin, 2026], or BalanceRAG-like cascaded-calibration machinery [Jia et al., 2026], recovers a U1-style certificate, and in the i.i.d. setting a joint finite-sample certificate for selection-conditioned risk and an acceptance floor is already available [Yu and Liu, 2026]; we therefore claim no novelty for the floor certificate itself, only for the four items above. Consistent with this scoping, our SCoRE-inspired betting arm (a risk-test ablation inside $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime } \mathbf { \bar { s } }$ construction, not a source-faithful SCoRE implementation) is valid and even modestly more powerful than Algorithm 1 in observed certification frequency (0 violations, certify-frequency $\geq \mathbf { B } ^ { \prime \prime } \mathbf { \ ` }$ s across all 1,024 audited cells at the same powered-cell count; §5.2, Appendix G): a finite-sample power advantage for the certificate is neither claimed nor needed. A separate i.i.d. selective conformal risk-control line combines confident-sample selection with conformal risk control (CRC) under exchangeability/PAC-style guarantees [Xu et al., 2025b]; it does not address bounded-ratio covariate shift, β-indexed lower bounds, or the labeled-source/unlabeled-target floor-certification map studied here. Table 1 (expanded row by row in Appendix E) compares at theorem level.

Selection-conditioned and ratio risk control. The accepted-answer ratio risk E[LS]/E[S] is now an active neighborhood (linear-expectation reformulation [Wang et al., 2025b], weighted e-values for general selective risks [Bai and Jin, 2026], cascaded lattice calibration [Jia et al., 2026], risk-aware routing with abstention [Hao et al., 2026]), with the lineage running back to selective classification and classification with a reject option [Chow, 1970, El-Yaniv and Wiener, 2010, Bartlett and Wegkamp, 2008, Geifman and El-Yaniv, 2017, Franc et al., 2023] and conformal selection, whose false discovery rate is itself a selected-set ratio [Jin and Candès, 2023a,b]. These certify whatever coverage the policy attains rather than a certified floor under shift; the reject-option formulations [Franc et al., 2023] do target a guaranteed-coverage (bounded-abstention) objective, but characterize the population optimum, without finite-sample floor-indexed certificates or rates under covariate shift.

Risk control under covariate shift. Importance weighting under covariate shift [Shimodaira, 2000] and weighted conformal prediction, a covariate-shift extension of conformal prediction [Vovk et al., 2022], introduced the density-ratio reweighting we build on [Tibshirani et al., 2019], extended beyond exchangeability [Barber et al., 2023] and to (high-probability) risk control under shift [Zecchin et al., 2025, Almeida et al., 2025], with direct ratio estimators and doubly robust, debiased, clipped, fine-grained, and kernel-matched weights making estimation practical [Kanamori et al., 2009, Yang et al., 2024, Kato et al., 2023, Wang and Goel, 2026, Laghuvarapu et al., 2026, Ai and Ren, 2024]. This family certifies marginal risk or coverage, never the selected-set ratio with a floor, with global ESS-type complexity rather than our localized functional.

Lower bounds and feasibility for distribution-free certification. A lower-bound culture exists: matching minimax bounds for non-monotone conformal risk control [Aldirawi et al., 2026], tight abstention bounds under adversarial injections [Edelman and Goel, 2026], overlap obstructions [D’Amour et al., 2021], prediction sets adaptive to unknown shift [Qiu et al., 2023], and tolerant learning with abstention [Goel et al., 2024]. All are floor-free: no β in any rate, hence no frontier, no two-resource (n, m) map. Our Claim-1 constructions live at bounded ratio, so they are not overlap results in disguise (Appendix B).

Learnability and estimability under arbitrary covariate shift. Closest in spirit to our impossibility leg are two lines asking when shift is tractable at all: reject-to-learn, where abstaining on unlabeled target points recovers efficient learnability under arbitrary covariate shift through a reliable-learner oracle [Kalai and Kanade, 2021]; and covariate-shifted mean estimation, which recovers E [f] for an unknown bounded f from labeled source and unlabeled target samples under structural conditions [Adil and Błasiok, 2026]. Our Model-B inconsistency (Theorem 6) is the certification analogue of the estimability boundary these navigate: over the unrestricted bounded-ratio class no (n, m)-uniform certificate exists, and the pre-registered K-cell Model-B<sup>′</sup> is the structural restriction that restores it, paralleling the structure those works impose. Neither subsumes floor-indexed certification (no coverage floor, no β-indexed rate, no risk–coverage frontier), but they locate why the floor is hard under shift.

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and cascade deployment. Certified RAG generation risk [Kang et al., 2024], conformal retrieval-augmented question answering [Li et al., 2024], conformal factuality [Mohri and Hashimoto, 2024], conformal abstention [Abbasi-Yadkori et al., 2024], and chance-constrained hallucination control [Mohandas, 2026] supply the loss substrate and baselines for our real-workload audit; the proof that confidence-based deferral fails under shift [Jitkrittum et al., 2023], together with selective question answering under domain shift via a learned calibrator [Kamath et al., 2020], supplies its motivation; none certify a coverage floor or price what the floor costs.

What is new here, exactly. The novelty is not the coverage floor as a population object (the reject-option line already studies guaranteed-coverage selective risk [Franc et al., 2023]) but the finite-sample theory the floor creates under covariate shift: β-indexed lower bounds, the two-resource (labeled/unlabeled) map, the localized accepted-region complexity, and the B<sup>′</sup> budgeted-nuisance analysis, not weighted testing, ratio linearization, or cascaded-RAG calibration, which we inherit and credit [Bates et al., 2021, Angelopoulos et al., 2021, 2024, Laufer-Goldshtein et al., 2023, Jia et al., 2026, Wang et al., 2025b, Tsybakov, 2009].

## 3 Problem, Setup, and the Certificate

## 3.1 Problem, notation, and the certify-or-refuse output

Labeled pairs $( X , Y ) \sim P$ carry a bounded loss $L \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ with conditional mean $\eta ( x ) = \mathbb { E } [ L \mid X = x ]$ ; the target supplies unlabeled covariates $\dot { X } ^ { \prime } \sim Q _ { X }$ . We assume covariate shift (the conditional map of $( Y ,$ outputs, L) given $X$ is identical under source and target) and a density ratio $w = d Q _ { X } / d P _ { X } \leq B$ ; write $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ for this class. Since $\textstyle \int w d P _ { X } = 1$ , membership forces $P _ { X } ( w > 0 ) \ge 1 / B$ : positivity holds throughout; no construction here uses unsupported target mass. A selective policy is a point λ on a pre-registered nested-threshold lattice Λ over a frozen router score, with acceptance indicator $\dot { S } _ { \lambda } \in \{ 0 , 1 \bar { \} }$ } (abstention and escalation mean $S _ { \lambda } = 0 )$ . The certified functionals are the selection-conditioned risk $R _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \dot { = } \dot { \mathbb { E } } _ { Q _ { X } } \lbrack \eta \dot { S } _ { \lambda } \rbrack / \mathbb { E } _ { Q _ { X } } \lbrack S _ { \lambda } \rbrack$ and the coverage $C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) = \operatorname { \mathbb { E } } _ { Q _ { X } } [ S _ { \lambda } ]$ . We adopt the convention $R _ { Q } ( \lambda ) : = + \infty$ when $C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) = 0 ,$ so a zero-coverage policy is never certifiable. Reject-all is thus infeasible and the frontier suprema below range over $\{ C _ { Q } > 0 \}$ ; escalate-all is a no-automatic-answer guarantee, not a success.

Definition 1 (Procedure, validity, and power). From n i.i.d. labeled draws from P and m i.i.d. unlabeled draws from $Q _ { X }$ (independent), a procedure A outputs a point of Λ or an honest refusal ⊥. It is valid at $( \alpha , \beta , \delta )$ if in every world $\mathbb { P } \big ( \pm \pm \bot \wedge ( R _ { Q } ( A ) > \alpha \vee C _ { Q } ( A ) < \beta ) \big ) \leq \delta ,$ and powered at a world if it certifies there with probability $\geq 1 / 2$

Table 2 collects the notation used throughout the paper.

## 3.2 The feasibility frontier and floor slack

Definition 2 (Budget functional and frontier). Let $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( u ) , u \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ , be the increasing rearrangement of $\eta$ under $Q _ { X }$ ; define the budget functional $\begin{array} { r } { \varphi ( c ) = \alpha c - \int _ { 0 } ^ { c } \eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( u ) } \end{array}$ du and the frontier $\beta ^ { * } ( \alpha , Q ) = \operatorname* { s u p } \{ c : \varphi ( c ) \geq 0 \}$ , the largest certifiable coverage under randomized acceptance (a fractional-knapsack value attained by the increasingrearrangement threshold rule, the generalized Neyman–Pearson solution [Dantzig and Wald, 1951]; for atomic $Q _ { X }$ the deterministic-selector frontier may be strictly smaller, Appendix A).

On a finite lattice the operative object is the lattice frontier $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } = \operatorname* { s u p } \{ C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) : \lambda \in \Lambda , R _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \leq \alpha \} \leq \beta ^ { * }$ We keep the two symbols distinct $\bar { ( \beta ^ { * } }$ for the relaxed frontier, $\hat { \beta } _ { \Lambda } ^ { \ast }$ for the operative lattice object; Figure 2b) and write $\bar { \Delta _ { \Lambda } } : = \beta ^ { * } - \bar { \beta } _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } \ge 0$ for the discretization gap, flagging any coarse-lattice construction where it is nonzero (Appendix $\mathbf { A } )$ . The map’s slack is the relaxed $s = \beta ^ { * } - \beta$ . We do not claim a bound uniform over a slack range on a single fixed lattice: the lower bound is existential in Λ (a compatible lattice supplied per $s ;$ Theorem 5, Corollary $^ { 2 ) }$ while a deployed fixed Λ certifies the operative slack $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } - \beta = \bar { s } - \Delta _ { \Lambda }$ . The inconsistency theorem is not existential in this sense: it fixes two absolute thresholds and holds on every lattice containing them (Theorem 6, step R4).

The population-infeasibility statements are for the lattice frontier $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * }$ , while the power theorems measure slack against the relaxed frontier, $s = \beta ^ { * } - \beta$ , and touch the finite lattice only through the explicit $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s )$ witness $\lambda _ { s }$ below.

The condition $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } }$ should not be read as implying $\beta ^ { * } = \beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * }$ . The two coincide up to grid resolution only under a sufficient lattice-design condition: a risk-score-aligned lattice whose coverage mesh (the largest gap between attainable coverages) i $s < s / 8$ and whose attainable coverages extend to within that mesh of the frontier $\beta ^ { * }$ itself. Fineness alone does not suffice, nor does reaching only the interior point $\beta ^ { * } - s / 2 \colon$ a grid stopping short of $\beta ^ { * }$ leaves $\beta ^ { * } - \beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * }$ as large as the terminal gap. By contrast, $\mathrm { L } \dot { \mathrm { R } } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s )$ alone needs only an interior witness near $\beta ^ { * } - s / 2 ,$ , so it can hold on a coarse lattice with $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } < \beta ^ { * }$ (Appendix A).

Definition 3 (Regular frontier margin). A world has a regularfrontier margin $( \kappa , s _ { 0 } ) \mathrm { i f } \varphi ( \beta ^ { * } ) = 0 , \kappa > 0 , 0 < s _ { 0 } \leq \beta ^ { * }$ and $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( u ) \geq \alpha + \kappa$ for a.e. $u \in [ \bar { \beta } ^ { * } - s _ { 0 } , \beta ^ { * } ]$

This margin is generic. With $\beta ^ { * } < 1$ , it fails only in the degenerate tie where $\varphi \equiv 0$ on $[ 0 , \beta ^ { * } ]$ (equivalently $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } = \alpha$ a.e. there), so any world with $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } < \alpha$ on a set of positive measure has the margin (Lemma 1). Under the margin, with floor slack $s = \beta ^ { * } ( \alpha , Q ) - { \ ' } \beta \in ( 0 , s _ { 0 } \wedge c _ { 0 } \beta ]$ (fixed $c _ { 0 } \in ( 0 , 1 ]$ , so the local regime has $s \leq \beta )$ , the risk budget is linear: $\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ) \geq$ κs (Figure $\mathrm { 2 a ) }$ . This single inequality drives both the bite divergence and the achievability margins (Appendix $\mathbf { A } , \mathbf { e q } . 2 )$ . Genericity is qualitative; it asserts a positive margin exists, not that it is large: κ and

The relaxed frontier is $\beta ^ { * } = \operatorname* { s u p } \{ c : \varphi ( c ) \geq 0 \}$ (Definition $2 ) ;$ in the depicted regular-margin case the areas balance there, $\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } ) = 0$ . The regular frontier margin (Definition 3) keeps $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } \geq \alpha + \kappa \mathrm { a . e . }$ . on $[ \beta ^ { * } - s _ { 0 } , \beta ^ { * } ]$ , so a floor $\beta = \beta ^ { * } - s$ in the local regime retains the linear reserve $\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ) \geq \kappa s$ (dashed rectangle), the single inequality behind both the bite divergence and the achievability margins. $s _ { 0 }$ may be arbitrarily small, and since the certificate’s sample complexity scales as $\kappa ^ { - 2 }$ (Theorem 7), a small frontier margin makes certification expensive without violating any assumption here.

Definition 4 (Lattice margin condition $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s ) )$ . On a finite lattice, $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s )$ holds if some $\lambda _ { s } \in \Lambda$ has coverage in $[ \beta + s / 4 , \beta ^ { * } - s / 4 ]$ ] and risk budget $\mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ S _ { \lambda } ( \alpha - \eta ) ] \ge \kappa s / 8$

We additionally assume $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s )$ on a finite lattice for the upper bounds; it is automatic for risk-score-aligned nested-threshold lattices whose coverage grid is finer than $s / 8$ and brackets $\beta ^ { * } - s / 2 ( \mathrm { A p p e n d i x \ A } )$ , while on a coarse lattice the certifiable slack plateaus within cells, and zero-margin cells drive the required n to infinity, a lattice-design diagnostic we also observe empirically (Appendices D, G.7).

## 3.3 Information models: A, B, and B<sup>′</sup>

The map is stated against a three-way model split, introduced once: A (oracle weights) receives w exactly: clean upper bound, rate reference; B (unknown weights) knows only $w \leq B \colon$ the lower-bound model, in which the floor is estimable label-free while the risk is not; $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ (estimable-ratio structure) knows $w \leq B$ plus a pre-registered K-cell partition on which w is constant: the implementable upper bound, with an explicit nuisance price.

## 3.4 Stratified-shift assumptions and data splits

Model-B<sup>′</sup> assumes, on top of $w \leq B ,$ , a shift model: a pre-registered finite partition $\mathcal { K } = \{ \mathcal { X } _ { 1 } , . . . , \mathcal { X } _ { K } \}$ of X on which w is constant: stratified covariate shift; the K numbers $w _ { k } = q _ { k } / p _ { k }$ are learned from finite samples at the explicit nuisance price below (K is a dial, not a disguise: Remark 17)

Assumption 1 (Stratified-shift certification setting). Conditions (i), (iii), (iv) are structural (class-defining); (ii) is an algorithm-feasibility requirement on the run’s labeled split size $n _ { w } ,$ , not a property of the world. (i) K is fixed before any data used by the run; $\mathrm { ( i i ) } p _ { k } = P _ { X } ( \mathcal { X } _ { k } ) \geq p _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } \geq 8 \log ( 4 K / \delta _ { w } ) / n _ { w }$ for all k, so K may grow only up to order $n _ { w } /$ log; (iii) each acceptance region $\{ S _ { \lambda } = 1 \}$ is a union of cells (K counts score-refined cells); (iv) $L \in [ 0 , 1 ]$

## 3.5 Algorithm: the budgeted floor-aware certificate

Algorithm 1 pairs an upper confidence bound (UCB) on the linearized risk with a lower confidence bound (LCB) on coverage, and certifies only when both clear their margins.

Algorithm 1 Budgeted floor-aware certificate (Model-B<sup>′</sup>)   
1: Split: source → $( D _ { w } ^ { P } , D _ { r } ) ;$ target $ ( D _ { w } ^ { Q } , D _ { f } )$ // four-block splitting   
2: $\hat { w } _ { k } \gets \mathrm { c l i p } ( \hat { q } _ { k } / \hat { p } _ { k } , 0 , B )$ on $D _ { w } \mathsf { \Gamma } ( \hat { w } _ { k } : = B \operatorname { i f } \hat { p } _ { k } = 0 )$ // K-cell histogram ratio   
3: $\begin{array} { r } { L _ { w } \gets \log \frac { 4 ( | \Lambda | + 1 ) } { \delta _ { w } } } \end{array}$ // simultaneity constant (loop-invariant)   
4: for $\lambda \in \Lambda ,$ one shared budget $\delta = \delta _ { w } + | \Lambda | \delta _ { r } ^ { \prime } + | \Lambda | \delta _ { f } ^ { \prime }$ do   
5: $\rho _ { \lambda }$ ← localized nuisance budget over λ’s accepted-cell masses (Proposition 1, via $\mathit { L } _ { w } ;$ plug-in corrected)   
6: risk-pass iff $\mathrm { U C B } _ { D _ { r } } \left( \hat { w } \cdot S _ { \lambda } \cdot ( L - \alpha ) \right) \le - \rho _ { \lambda }$ // budgeted linear test (empirical Bernstein)   
7: floor-pass iff $\mathrm { L C B } _ { D _ { f } } \left( S _ { \lambda } \right) \ge \beta$ and $> 0$ // weight-free floor (Bernstein)   
8: end for   
9: Output: the floor-LCB-maximal λ passing both (ties broken by larger risk-UCB slack, then earliest lattice index); else ⊥ with   
observable test-side attribution (priority and optional diagnoses in §4.5)

Algorithm 1 linearizes the ratio constraint at fixed α [Wang et al., 2025b] and tests it with a Maurer–Pontil empirical-Bernstein UCB [Maurer and Pontil, 2009] at margin $- \rho _ { \lambda }$ , which absorbs the weight-estimation error; the floor LCB never touches weights, and selection among simultaneously valid tests is free in the learn-then-test pattern [Angelopoulos et al., 2021, Laufer-Goldshtein et al., 2023]. The shared confidence budget allocates $\delta _ { w }$ to weight estima tion and $\bar { \delta } _ { r } ^ { \prime } , \delta _ { f } ^ { \prime }$ to each of the |Λ| per-λ risk and floor tests. The displayed budget $\rho _ { \lambda }$ is the radius the power analysis is proved for; the released implementation computes a different registered closed form, valid at the same $\delta _ { w }$ and used for every reported $\mathbf { B ^ { \prime } }$ certification frequency and by the SCoRE arm that shares the radius, stated and proved in Remark 14.

## 4 The Floor Certification Map: Guarantees and Limits

## 4.1 Main result: the map theorem

The map is a model-split statement, not a single-model law. Because Model-B is inconsistent over the full unknown-weight class $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ (Theorem 6), the three rates match across models (a Model-A oracle-weight upper, the Model-B lower, and a restricted Model-B<sup>′</sup> implementable upper) rather than as a single-model minimax law. We state the three claims first; their precise scope (where the match is cross-model versus within-Model-B<sup>′</sup>, and the exact status of the nuisance necessity) is collected in Remark 1 immediately after the theorem. The three claims refine the headline law (1); per-claim model (Models $\mathbf { A } / \mathbf { B } / \mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ of §3.3), assumptions, and status are summarized in Table 3, and Figure 3 draws the architecture of the match

Theorem 1 (The Floor Certification Map). Standing assumptions: fix risk level $\alpha \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ , confidence $\delta \leq 1 / 8 ,$ bounded ratio $w \leq B _ { \mathrm { \scriptsize { : } } }$ , and a regularfrontier margin $( \kappa , s _ { 0 } ) ,$ ; let thefloor β have slack $s = \bar { \beta } ^ { * } ( \bar { \alpha } , Q ) - \beta \in ( 0 ,$ , s<sub>0</sub> $\wedge c _ { 0 } \beta ]$ (the local regime). The upper bounds (Claims 2–3) additionallyfix a nested-threshold lattice Λ with $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s ) ;$ ; the lower bound (Claim 1) is existential in the lattice: for each s the hard instance supplies its own compatible Λ (Theorem 5), so the match is not uniform over a singlefixed Λ (Corollary 2). The Model-B inconsistency theorem is not existential in this sense: it holds on every lattice containing twofixed absolute thresholds (Theorem 6).

Claim 1: lower (Model-B: unknown weights, only $w \le B )$ . On the parameter rectangle $B \geq 2 , \alpha \in [ 0 . 2 , 0 . 9 ]$ $\beta \in ( 0 , 0 . 6 ] , \kappa < \frac { 1 } { 2 }$ min{α, 1 − α} ofTheorem 5 (the non-vacuous regime; the lower-bound constants are uniform over $B \geq 2 ,$ , since a larger ratio bound only enlarges $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ and strengthens the impossibility), no valid procedure can certify every slack-s world once s $\lesssim \kappa ^ { - 1 } \sqrt { \beta / n } + \sqrt { \beta / m } .$ : two necessary conditions witnessed inside one constructionfamily $p e r \left( n , m \right)$ , whose maxform yields the additive display up to afactor of2 (each axis separately on the general class).

Figure 3: The Floor Certification Map as a level diagram (Theorem 1). Vertical axis: floor slack s at fixed $( n , m )$ ; the dashed level is the two-axis rate $\kappa ^ { - 1 } \sqrt { \beta / n } + \sqrt { \beta / m }$ of the template (1), and hatching marks regions with no uniform guarantee: some hard world is refused with probability at least $\frac { 1 } { 2 }$ there. Model B (unknown weights, only $w \leq B ) ;$ : below the level refusal is forced on a hard world (Claim 1, Theorem $5 ;$ one hard construction family per $( n , m )$ , existential in the lattice with a compatible Λ per s); moreover uniform power fails already at the fixed constant witness slack $\begin{array} { r } { s _ { * } = \frac { 1 } { 4 } } \end{array}$ for every $( n , m )$ , since Model B is inconsistent over $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ at the operating point $\alpha = \beta = \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } ( B \geq 2 , \delta \leq 1 / 8 ,$ , on every finite nested-threshold lattice containing the theorem’s two fixed thresholds; Theorem 6), so no shrinking uniform certifiable threshold exists and no certifiable region is asserted for this column. Model A (oracle weights): the budgeted certificate certifies above the same level up to constants and logarithms (Claim 2, Theorem $^ { 7 ) ; }$ this shared level is the cross-model match, while no matching Model-A floor-axis lower bound is claimed and the below-level region is left uncharacterized (Remark 9). Model $B ^ { \prime }$ (estimated weights on a pre-registered K-cell partition): certifies above the level plus the explicitly priced nuisance increment $\rho _ { \lambda } / \kappa$ (Claim 3, Theorems 2–3); the lower bound re-runs within $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ under a compatible partition and lattice pre-registered for the target s (not uniform in s or over arbitrary partitions), and together with the upper bound it yields a correspondence on the totals when the weight splits do not dominate, pointwise and instance-dependent rather than a uniform minimax (Corollary 2); nuisance necessity is only partial: K-free core necessary, histogram $B ^ { 2 } K$ rate only sufficient, unknown-η open (Remark 1, Theorem $8 ) .$ The map is therefore necessarily model-split, and the cross-model match is the correct object rather than a substitute for an in-class theorem. Per-claim assumptions and precise scope are collected in Table 3.

Claim 2: upper (Model-A: oracle weights). A floor-aware budgeted certificate (margin-bearing lattice) certifies every slack-s world once $n \gtrsim \beta \log ( | \Lambda | / \delta ) / ( \kappa s ) ^ { 2 }$ and m $\gtrsim \beta \log ( | \Lambda | / \delta ) / s ^ { 2 } .$ : the same two axes (atfixed bounded-ratio constant B, folded into the constants; the explicit B-dependent variance and range terms are in Theorem 7); the A-upper attains the B-lower’s rates up to constants and logarithms (a cross-model match).

Claim 3: upper (Model-B<sup>′</sup>: estimated weights under a pre-registered K-cell stratified shift). The same certificate with a clipped histogram ratio and localized nuisance budget $\rho _ { \lambda }$ certifies once additionally $\rho _ { \lambda } \lesssim$ κs: a sufficient nuisance condition, not a proved-necessary axis (schematically $n _ { w } \stackrel { . . . } { \wedge } m _ { w } \stackrel { . } { \sim } B ^ { 2 } K \cdot \mathrm { m a s s } ( A _ { \lambda } ) \stackrel { . } { / } ( \stackrel { . . . } { \kappa } s ) ^ { 2 } ,$ , with the precise per-split $m _ { w }$ and $n _ { w }$ conditions given by Theorem 3). Validity is conditional on the stratified-shift model, and the nuisance’s necessity is only partially settled: a K-free core necessary, the histogram $B ^ { 2 } K$ rate only sufficient, the unknown-η case open (Remark 1, Theorem 8, Appendix H).

Remark 1 (Scope of the map). Cross-model vs. within-model. The match is across models (Model-A upper vs. Model-B lower) over the full class. On the two resource axes it additionally holds within Model-B<sup>′</sup>, as an instance-dependent (pointwise-on-totals) rate correspondence under a compatible pre-registered partition, not a uniform minimax over the class, and only a componentwise correspondence on the test sub-blocks $( n _ { r } , m _ { f } )$ (Remark 5, Corollary 2), leaving only the histogram nuisance axis cross-model.

Nuisance necessity (partial). The $\mathbf { B ^ { \prime } }$ nuisance is the histogram estimator’s price, not a proved-necessary axis. Feasibility is p-independent: the linearized risk $G _ { Q } ( \lambda ) = \mathbb { E } _ { P } [ w \bar { S _ { \lambda } } ( L - \alpha ) ]$ equals $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { k : \mathcal { K } _ { k } \subseteq A _ { \lambda } } q _ { k } \mathopen { } \mathclose \bgroup \left( \bar { \eta } _ { k } - \alpha \aftergroup \egroup \right) } \end{array}$ under the partition $( { \bar { \eta } } _ { k } = \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ \eta \mid X \in { \mathcal { X } } _ { k } ] )$ , a sum over accepted cells with no separate $p _ { k }$ , so no lower bound forces a separate population source-covariate (p-estimation) split. The necessary core is K-free (lattice-valued certification, the standing output model of §3): a target $\Omega ( q ( A ) / { s } ^ { 2 } )$ ), the floor m-axis localized, proved in the known-η submodel of Theorem $^ { 8 , }$ plus the labeled n-axis varying-η reduction. The histogram $B ^ { 2 } K$ rate is only sufficient; only the unknown-η case stays open, with no complete minimax characterization claimed (Appendix H).

Table 3: The three claims of the Floor Certification Map (Theorem 1) by information model. Standing assumptions (all claims): confidence $\delta \ \leq \ 1 / 8 ,$ , bounded ratio w $\leq B ,$ , a nested-threshold lattice with $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } }$ (pre-registered for the upper bounds; constructed per s for the lower), a regular frontier margin $\left( \kappa , s _ { 0 } \right)$ , and floor slack in the local regime $s = \beta ^ { * } - \beta \in ( 0 , s _ { 0 } \wedge c _ { 0 } \beta ]$ The match is across models (B-lower vs. A-upper) over the full class, and additionally within Model- ${ \bf - B ^ { \prime } }$ on the two resource axes (instance-dependent, not a uniform minimax over the class; the nuisance axis stays cross-model); the precise scope is Remark 1 (formally Corollary 2). The Model-A side is shown achievable but a matching Model-A floor-axis lower bound is not claimed (Remark 9).

Geometry. The upper-bound variance proxy and the lower-bound hard-slice construction both localize to the accepted region and point to the localized second-moment family $\mathbb { E } _ { P } [ w ^ { 2 } S _ { \lambda } ]$ (an accepted-region quantity, not global ESS) as the operative complexity proxy in this analysis (Remark 11; its normalized effective-sample-size reading $\frac { \mathbb { E } _ { P } \left[ w ^ { 2 } S _ { \lambda } \right] } { ( \mathbb { E } _ { P } \left[ w S _ { \lambda } \right] ) ^ { 2 } }$ is heuristic, not separately derived here). This identification rests on the matched bound structure and the family-1 experiments (§5.1); a standalone theorem separating two worlds at fixed global ESS and frontier is left open, as is exact two-sided constant matching (Remark 11).

Remark 2 (The map is floor-created) $\mathbf { A s } \beta  0$ both lower-bound axes in Claim 1 vanish, each carrying ${ \sqrt { \beta } } ,$ in the joint local regime $s \leq c _ { 0 } \beta ,$ , so the slack vanishes with $\beta$ (a vestigial sample requirement to confirm positive coverage persists). This vanishing is of the certifiable absolute slack threshold $s \lesssim \kappa ^ { - 1 } \sqrt { \beta / n } + \sqrt { \beta / m }$ at fixed $( n , m )$ ; holding the relative slack $s / \beta$ fixed instead, the requirements n $\gtrsim \beta / ( \kappa s ) ^ { 2 }$ , m $\gtrsim \beta / s ^ { 2 }$ grow as $\Omega ( 1 / \beta )$ , so certifying a fixed relative slack does not become free as $\beta  0 .$ . Meanwhile $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ and its overlap structure are unchanged: the positivity non-identifiability of D’Amour et al. [2021] persists at $\beta = 0$ , and the s-scaled hardness exists only because the floor forces acceptance up to the frontier; Appendix B.6 draws the contrasting overlap-created boundary. The map is created by the floor, not by overlap (Remarks 7, 8); the registered consistency diagnostic (monotone vanishing of the smallest certifiable slack as $\beta  0 )$ is Figure A3 (Appendix G.5).

Formal versions: Theorem 5 (Claim 1), Theorem 7 (Claim 2, phase-diagram corollaries), Theorems 2–3/Proposition 1 (Claim 3; the parenthetical nuisance display above is schematic; the per-split form is Theorem $3 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { s } ) ;$ assembled in Appendix C, pilot exponent artifact resolved in Appendix D. Each leg is elaborated in turn below.

## 4.2 Model-B two-axis lower bound, and no matching Model-B upper

Claim 1 is the impossibility leg; its formal statement is Theorem 5 (Appendix B). Over the full unknown-weight class there is no matching Model-B upper: Model-B is inconsistent over $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ , with a hard world at every $( n , m )$ at the operating point $\begin{array} { r } { \alpha = \beta = \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \end{array}$ (Theorem 6, Appendix B.4). This inconsistency is why the map is necessarily model-split, not a defect of the analysis: a single-model minimax statement over $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ is provably unattainable (no valid procedure certifies a constant-slack world at any sample size), so the operative question is not the (nonexistent) $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ minimax rate but which complexity restriction restores consistency and at what price, answered by Model-A (oracle weights) and Model-B<sup>′</sup> (a pre-registered partition). The restriction is thus load-bearing, and the cross-model match is the correct object, not a substitute for an in-class theorem that cannot exist (Figure 3).

Proof sketch. (i) The n-axis pair needs no shift $( w \equiv 1 ) \colon$ raising the loss on an inframarginal slice (mass $\rho = \beta / 2 )$ by $\Delta = 4 \kappa s / \beta$ flips every coverage-≥ β threshold to infeasible while the unlabeled law is untouched, at per-sample Kullback–Leibler (KL) cost $\lesssim \rho \Delta ^ { 2 } \asymp ( \kappa s ) ^ { 2 } / \beta ;$ so Le Cam forces refusal once $s \lesssim \kappa ^ { - 1 } \sqrt { \beta / n }$ . (ii) The m-axis pair inverts the channels: shared $( P _ { X } , \eta ) , Q _ { X }$ moves mass $\varepsilon \asymp s$ out of the safe block at $\chi ^ { 2 }$ cost ≍ $\varepsilon ^ { 2 } / \beta$ per draw, so the $\sqrt { \beta }$ is intrinsic, matching the variance-aware floor-LCB rate $\sqrt { \beta ( 1 - \beta ) / m }$ on the achievability side (Appendix B.2). (iii)

Additive combination: both pairs share the base world, so one family per $( n , m )$ witnesses both axes, and the maximum of the two widths is at least half their sum: the additive display is this max form up to a factor of 2 (Appendix B.3).

## 4.3 Model-A oracle achievability and the phase diagram

Claim 2’s oracle-weight certificate (Theorem 7, Appendix C) attains the Claim-1 rates up to constants and logarithms, the cross-model match.

Proof sketch. Model-A achievability: an empirical-Bernstein test of the linearized risk $G _ { Q } ( \lambda ) = \mathbb { E } _ { P } [ w S _ { \lambda } ( L - \alpha ) ] \le$ 0 [Maurer and Pontil, 2009] plus a variance-aware floor LCB, one shared budget over Λ [Angelopoulos et al., 2021], certifies once $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s ) \mathrm { \bar { s } }$ margins $( \kappa s / 8$ risk, $s / 4$ floor) absorb the deviations.

Corollary 1 (Phase diagram). At slack s the sufficient guaranteed-certifiable region in the $( n , m )$ plane (the set on which Theorem $7 3$ thresholds are met) contains a quadrant up to constants; in Model-A the source-weightedfloor branch enlarges it (the labeled n-route can clear thefloor even when the m-axis is starved), so the region is the union of the m-route quadrant $\{ n \ge N _ { \mathrm { r i s k } } , m \ge M _ { \mathrm { f l o o r } } \}$ and an n-only half-strip $\{ n \ge \operatorname* { m a x } ( N _ { \mathrm { r i s k } } , N _ { \mathrm { f l o o r } } ) \}$ (thresholds in Theorem 7). Its corner is a sufficient-guarantee boundary (schematic up to the unknown ≳-constants, not a tight necessity threshold) indicating which purchase extends the guarantee: left of it, buy labeled source; below it, buy unlabeled target; when the nuisance axis binds, buy weight-block samples $n _ { w } , m _ { w }$ (the nuisance radius shrinks at Proposition 1’s rate); only shift-model misspecification (weights not K-measurable) is uncurable by samples (sufficient boundary: Corollary 4; feasibility readout: Corollary 3).

On the algorithm’s test-sample sub-blocks $( n _ { r } , m _ { f } )$ alone the correspondence is weaker: a componentwise rate match (the test terms carry the same $\beta / ( \kappa s ) ^ { 2 } , \ \beta / s ^ { 2 }$ dependence as the total-sample lower bounds), not a minimax lower bound on $( n _ { r } , m _ { f } )$ themselves. $$

This sample-size comparison is distinctfrom the accuracy condition $\rho _ { \lambda _ { s } } \leq \kappa s / 3 2$ . One pre-registered $( \mathcal { K } , \Lambda )$ serves both bounds, reconciling Theorem 5’s after-s lattice with Theorem 3’s pre-registered one (proof: Appendix F).

$\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } }$ is likewise a substantive practical assumption: risk-score-aligned lattices inherit it (Appendix C); a misaligned score can leave the lattice margin-free; then the certificate refuses, and the refusal is observable, though its test-side label does not by itself identify the missing lattice margin as the cause.

## 4.5 Nuisance necessity, the open edge, and what $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ does not certify

Validity in Theorem 2 is conditional on the pre-registered K-cell stratified-shift model, the same epistemic status as the covariate-shift assumption itself, with the added pre-registered K-measurability stated here, and we map its boundary rather than assume it away: if w is not K-measurable, the guarantee degrades to the K-projection of w with an additive bias term not estimable from covariates alone (Remark 16). On the real workload the $\ S 5 . 3$ sensitivity experiment shows refusal-status invariance only, not correctness of the shift model. Separately, a deliberately adversarial synthetic sweep (Appendix G.9) constructs a within-cell $w { - } \eta$ correlation that stays in $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ but, by design, leaves the $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime } \dot { \boldsymbol { \kappa } }$ -measurable class. That perturbation is therefore invisible to $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime } \mathbf { \bar { s } }$ own certificate, and it nonetheless drives true violations once the receding frontier crosses the locked operating point, while oracle-A stays valid: an adversarial characterization of the conditional guarantee’s boundary, not an off-partition robustness guarantee, which the paper does not claim. The nuisance-axis necessity is only partially settled (Theorem 8, Appendix H): a K-free core necessary (lattice-valued) in the known-η submodel (a localized restatement of the m-axis target bound plus the existing n-axis reduction; the worst-slice $B ^ { 2 } p ( A )$ envelope stated, not fully re-derived), histogram $B ^ { 2 } K$ not proved necessary, unknown-η the one open edge, no complete characterization claimed.

Honest-failure semantics. A refusal ⊥ carries a deterministic axis attribution: two labels the certificate itself computes (risk-starved, floor-starved) and two conditional diagnoses (nuisance-limited, frontier-infeasible). For the data-starved labels, Corollary 1 reads off what to buy under that reading [Kato et al., 2023, Yang et al., 2024, Wang and Goel, 2026, Laghuvarapu et al., 2026, Wang et al., 2025a], while when a separate frontier diagnosis supports frontier infeasibility, no n or m purchase fixes the refusal (raise capability or renegotiate the service-level agreement). Call thefloor-pass set those $\lambda \in \Lambda$ whose floor LCB is at least β and positive, exactly the floor-pass set Algorithm 1 computes. When several attributions bind at once the report follows a fixed priority: nuisance-limited $( \rho _ { \lambda } > \kappa s / 3 2$ at the floor-LCB-maximal candidate) ≻ risk-starved (the floor-pass set is nonempty but no member clears the risk UCB) ≻ floor-starved (the floor-pass set is empty); frontier-infeasible is reported in place of these only when the separate frontier diagnosis (§5.3) certifies $\beta ^ { * } < \beta ;$ this convention fixes only the displayed reason and never alters the certify/refuse decision or its validity. The labels differ in what they require. Risk-starved and floor-starved are read off the certificate’s own statistics, since both the floor-pass set and the risk test are computed inside Algorithm 1. The nuisance-limited comparison additionally involves $( \kappa , s )$ , which the algorithm does not receive and which the data does not identify, so it can be reported only where those are supplied as planning values; no run reported here uses it, and absent it a refusal is displayed as risk-starved or floor-starved by the priority above. Frontier-infeasible likewise comes from the separate diagnosis of §5.3, not from the certificate’s own output. A risk-starved label can therefore stand in for an undiagnosed nuisance-limited refusal, so the purchase guidance of Corollary 1 is conditional on the displayed reading. Escalate-all rows are reported as a no-automatic-answer guarantee, never as a win.

## 5 Experimental Evaluation

We evaluate the map on registered synthetic generators (an audit of validity, practical constants, and mechanism consistency; §5.1, §5.2) and then audit the certificate’s honest refusal on a real workload (§5.3).

## 5.1 Synthetic mechanisms and rate-shape evidence

This suite is an audit of the map on registered generators for validity, practical constants, and mechanism consistency: theorem-carried evidence, not a claim of broad empirical generality for the $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ certificate; the scope guard is part of each claim. Every block below is claim-driven, with protocol and registered generators in Appendix G.1 and number-to-file traceability maintained through the authors’ frozen results manifest (reporting pipeline: Appendix G.11). One reporting discipline holds throughout: $\delta = 0 . 0 5$ (certificate confidence, pooled criterion), $\delta _ { \mathrm { t o l } } = 0 . 0 6 2 5$ (powered per-cell tolerance), and 95% Clopper–Pearson upper confidence bounds (CP-UCB) on violation rates [Clopper and Pearson, 1934] are three separate quantities, never interchanged, with Holm’s correction [Holm, 1979] for multiplicity. For

Bite divergence near the boundary (Claim C1). In the registered synthetic bite family, the labeled-sample requirement diverges quadratically near the certifiable boundary, exactly as the map’s labeled axis predicts: required-n vs. slack log–log slope −2.002, 95% CI [−2.142, −1.862] ⊂ pre-registered band $[ - 2 . 3 , - 1 . { \bar { 7 } } ]$ ; the curvature CI contains 0 (Figure 4). The fit rests on 9 slack design points; a deterministic pairs bootstrap (20,000 resamples) gives a 95% slope $\operatorname { C I } \left[ - 2 . 1 1 , - 1 . 8 9 \right]$ , consistent with the OLS normal-approximation interval, with log-space residuals $\leq 0 . 1 0$ in magnitude (raw points and bootstrap script in Appendix G). Breadth is now measured, not deferred: the labeled bite holds across three structurally distinct families: continuum (the registered regular-margin fam $\mathrm { { l y , - 2 . 0 0 2 } }$ , in band), kcell $( - 1 . 6 1 7 , \mathrm { C I } \left[ - 1 . 7 6 , - 1 . 4 7 \right] )$ , and a non-theorem-aligned family $\bar { ( - 1 . 8 4 5 , C I [ - 2 . 0 2 , - 1 . 6 7 ] ) }$ ; the steep, near-quadratic divergence is robust across all three (slopes in $[ - 2 . 0 , - 1 . 6 ] \rangle$ ). The exact −2 is specific to the regular-margin family. The exponent ordering tracks the accepted-region margin geometry (margin-vs-slack exponent $\bar { b } \approx 1 . 1 0 / \mathrm { 0 . 9 1 } / \mathrm { 0 . 9 \dot { 9 } }$ for continuum/kcell/non-theorem), consistent with the localized-functional thesis (Claim C4); the precise exponent additionally reflects finite-sample range terms and lattice discreteness. The relaxed-crossing/lattice-plateau dichotomy behind this ordering, together with the separation of the empirical margin-vs-slack b from the relaxed budget’s linear frontier-margin slope, is given by Proposition 3 with Lemma 3 (Appendix D); the finite-sample record is in Appendix G.

Two-axis form and contours: consistency checks, not proof (Claims C2, C3). The two-axis functional form itself is carried by Theorem 1; the experiments here are consistency checks. Held-out cross-validation (CV) of candidate $s ^ { * } ( n , m )$ surfaces gives the additive model the best point estimate (surface CV $R ^ { 2 } = 0 . 9 2 0 2 )$ . At the registered density (a 41-cell surface) the CI-separation criterion failed (bootstrap CIs overlapped) and the axis-exponent CIs missed containment, so we reported the form as theorem-carried and registered the failure as sample-limited; a denser surface bears that out. Re-running the same registered model-comparison procedure (byte-identical criterion; only design density and Monte-Carlo depth raised) at increasing cell count, the probability of strict non-overlap against every alternative rises 0.12 (40-cell base rung, the sweep resolution nearest the registered $4 1 )  0 . 7 5 ( 1 3 4 )  \mathrm { \bar { 1 . 0 0 } } ( 6 3 5 $ cells, 800 reps). At 635 cells the additive form (held-out cross-validation root-mean-square error, CV-RMSE 0.0122, 95% CI $[ 0 . 0 1 \bar { 0 9 } , 0 . 0 1 3 4 ] \rangle$ separates from all four candidate forms (the nearest 2.2× worse), unanimously across all 24 re-splits, with a monotonically growing gap (Figure A2; Appendix G.4). The CI-separation criterion is thus met and the 41-cell failure was statistical power, not non-identifiability (the per-axis exponent-containment sub-test is a separate check, not revisited here); the $R ^ { \hat { 2 } } = 0 . 9 2 0 2$ is the $s ^ { * } ( n , m )$ surface-CV result, not contour evidence. Contour transfer is qualitative rate-shape only: two-axis constants fit on the first shift intensity and applied unchanged give in-sample L1 correlation .997 and held-out L2–L4 correlations .981/.963/.890, but the strict pre-registered ±3% bands failed at all intensities, with held-out median factor errors $\times 1 . 2 4 / \times 1 . 9 1 / \times 3 . 4 0 ( \mathrm { F i g u r e \ : \bar { A } 1 } )$ : constants do not transfer; ordering and rate shape do. The non-transfer is itself systematic, not noise: the factor errors are strictly monotone in shift intensity (rank correlation 1.0) and grow ×3.2 from L1 (in-sample, ×1.07) to L4 (×3.40) while the accepted-region variance proxy grows only ×1.3: a predictable, intensity-driven constant inflation with rate-shape preserved (log-correlation $\ge . 8 8 9$ at every intensity), not an unstructured breakdown (Appendix G.2; numeric record in Appendix G.9).

Localized geometry, restricted (Claim C4). The geometry reading of Theorem 1 (Remark 11; the normalized form is heuristic, not separately derived) suggests that certification cost tracks the localized accepted-region functional $\frac { \mathbb { E } _ { P } \left[ w ^ { 2 } S _ { \lambda } \right] } { ( \mathbb { E } _ { P } \left[ w S _ { \lambda } \right] ) ^ { 2 } }$ rather than global ESS, and the $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ arm of family 1 shows this cleanly: across 640 worlds, $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ required-n has Spearman .936 (95% bootstrap CI [.927, .944]) against the localized functional and .026 against global ESS (Figure 5). The restriction is part of the claim: empirical support is limited to $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ family 1 plus qualified family-2 within-pair evidence (within-pair ratio agreement .995 but pooled Spearman .503 under κ/censoring confounds, 162/640 worlds censored), and oracle-arm pooled correlations are not supportive (a range-constant artifact; Appendix G.3); no broad-class claim is made.

(a) $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ required n against the localized functional $E _ { P } [ w ^ { 2 } S _ { \lambda } ] \dot { / } ( E _ { P } [ w S _ { \lambda } ] ) ^ { 2 } \mathrm { : }$ Spearman .936, 95% bootstrap CI [.927, .944]. ## 5.2 Validity audit and practical constants

In the full B1 grid audit (4 intensities $\times \ 2 5 6$ cells, 1,000 replications each, with five higher-variance edge cells re-run at 2,000–8,000), the formal-guarantee arms are valid where they certify: oracle-A certifies 583/1,024 cells and $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ certifies 8/1,024, for 591 arm-cell certifications in total. Those 591 certifications carry 0 violations, 0 Holm rejections, pooled $\mathrm { C P \mathrm { - } U C B s ~ 9 . 0 \times 1 0 ^ { - 6 } } / 1 . 8 \times 1 0 ^ { - 4 } \leq \delta$ , and powered per-cell $\mathrm { C P - U C B s } \le \mathrm { \bar { \delta } } _ { \mathrm { t o l } }$ (Table 4; Figure 1b). These are the released kernels’ runs: Remark 14 states the nuisance radius the $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ kernel computes and proves the guarantee for it, and Remark 10 states the two-family variant the oracle-A kernel runs and proves its validity at the same δ. A deep-audit subset (6 cells × 4,000 replications, both formal arms) recorded 0 violations in 48,000 replications, pooled CP-UCB $6 . 2 \times 1 0 ^ { - 5 }$ . Two denominators appear in this paper and we label each interval by the one it uses. The unconditional convention divides by the replication count and bounds $\mathbb { P } ($ (some invalid λ is certified), which is the quantity the $( \alpha , \beta , \delta )$ guarantee caps at $\delta ;$ the conditional convention divides by the number of certifying replications and bounds the violation rate among issued certificates. Every interval in the B1 grid audit and in the deep-audit subset just quoted is unconditional, including the 48,000 above, so none of them is a rate among issued certificates. The one place we report the conditional convention is the targeted existence run below, where the denominator is written explicitly as certifying replications. As a fairness check on the lower-bound thesis we substitute the risk test inside $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime \dagger } \mathbf { \hat { s } }$ exact construction: a SCoRE-inspired floor-augmented betting arm, in the spirit of Bai and Jin [2026], in which a weighted betting e-value replaces the empirical-Bernstein risk test and every other block is verbatim-identical to $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ (Proposition 5). This is an equal-budget risk-test ablation, not a source-faithful SCoRE implementation: Bai and Jin [2026] builds conformal risk-adjusted e-values for individual test instances and thresholds them, whereas this arm keeps $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime } \mathbf { \bar { s } }$ weight estimator, nuisance radius, floor LCB, four-block split, and output rule and bets on the calibration block. We therefore do not present it as the strongest available same-target baseline, but as the equal-construction alternative we tested against $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime \dagger } \mathbf { s }$ risk test, which is what the lower-bound thesis needs. It is valid and competitive: across the 1,024-cell grid its certify-frequency $\mathrm { i s } \geq \mathbf { B } ^ { \prime \prime } \mathrm { s }$ in every cell (strictly greater in $^ { 8 ) }$ at the same powered-cell count, with 0 violations. In this fixed matched-replication run it therefore weakly dominates $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ in observed certify-frequency without enlarging the powered set; we report no paired test or interval for that ordering, so it is an ordering observed in this Monte Carlo run rather than an estimated power gap. That an otherwise verbatim-identical alternative shares $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime \prime } \mathbf { \bar { s } }$ conservatism is the evidence we want: the conservatism is a shared price of the matched estimated-weight construction, not specific to the empirical-Bernstein risk test. This is exactly why the certificate’s contribution is the lower-bound theory and the priced nuisance, not a finite-sample power edge (full head-to-head and pre-registration: the SCoRE head-to-head record of Appendix G.9; self-contained validity proof: Appendix F.5). Demonstration arms violate at scale: floor-free certification draws 108 Holm rejections, weighted conformal [Tibshirani et al., 2019, Barber et al., 2023] draws 40, and plug-in draws 662; the distributionally robust optimization (DRO)-box baseline certifies 0 cells: an escalate-all, no-automatic-answer guarantee, not validity evidence (Proposition 4, Appendix F.4).

This is also a practical-constants result, and we keep the constants visible: $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ is much more conservative than oracle-A: at $\kappa s = 0 . 0 4 2$ , oracle-A first certifies at $n _ { \mathrm { t o t a l } } = 4 { , } 0 9 6$ and fully certifies at 16,384, $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ at 524,288 and 1,048,576 (128× at the first-certification onset, 64× at full certification); the registered K-sweep prices the shift-model dial directly (Table 4, bottom). These multipliers are measured against the released two-family oracle kernel (Remark 10), which differs from $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ in its confidence allocation and floor construction as well as in knowing w, so they bundle those differences with weight estimation rather than isolating it; read them as the cost of the whole estimated-weight construction, an upper bound on the weight-estimation cost alone, and not as an artifact of the risk test [Wang and Goel, 2026, Kimura and Hino, 2024]. Isolating weight estimation would need a comparator matched to $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ in every block except the weights, which we do not run.

These constants are one slice of a characterized envelope, not an isolated harsh point: $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ certifies across the partition sweep $K \in \{ 4 , \ldots , 6 4 \}$ with an onset premium over oracle-A that scales linearly in the partition dimension $\dot { ( K ^ { 0 . 9 5 5 } }$ 95% CI [0.755, 1.154] ∋ 1), while the declared bound B is second-order and the $\mathbf { \bar { B } ^ { \prime } }$ labeled onset obeys the same $s ^ { - 2 }$ bite as oracle-A. This is exactly the reading of Theorem 8: this K-scaling premium is the histogram estimator’s $\ell ^ { 1 }$ weight-recovery price (Proposition 1), not a necessary certification cost; the necessary core is K-free (lattice-valued) and matched from below (full envelope, with the $B ^ { 0 . { \breve { 6 } } 2 }$ and $s ^ { - 2 }$ records: Appendices H, G).

A targeted companion run confirms $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime } \mathbf { \bar { s } }$ conservatism is not an all-refusal failure mode: on one on-class $K = 4 \mathrm { g r i d } , \mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ reaches certify-frequency 1.0 at $n = m = 2 6 2 .$ ,144 $( s = 0 . 2 0 )$ and $n = m = 1 , 0 4 8 , 5 7 6 \ : ( s = 0 . 1 2 )$ , with $0 / 1 6 { \overset { - } { , } } 2 9 7 \mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ certifying replications violating: positive existence evidence only, not a breadth or real-workload claim (Appendix G)

## 5.3 Real-workload negative feasibility audit

This section reports a negative feasibility audit (it exercises the certificate’s refusal semantics on real data), not a second validation block, and not a deployment demonstration; the service-level agreement (SLA) is a pre-registered operational scenario, fixed before any data was described (not an external mandate or benchmark), and the scope is a single-corpus domain-shift audit.

Setup. The workload is extractive question answering under one domain-shift axis: SQuAD as calibration source, NewsQA as shifted target, both via the MRQA distribution [Rajpurkar et al., 2016, Trischler et al., 2017, Fisch et al., 2019], $N _ { \mathrm { e v a l } } = 4 { , } 2 1 2$ evaluation items. Correctness is non-judge (token-level $\mathrm { F 1 \ge 0 . 5 }$ against gold answers, avoiding model-judge biases [Zheng et al., 2023]), selection scores follow standard uncertainty families [Farquhar et al., 2024], the pipeline is frozen and cached, and the SLA scenario was fixed pre-data at $\alpha = . 1 0 , \beta = . 6 0 , \delta = . 0 5$ . Two capability legs run on the same corpus: leg 1 Llama-3-8B [Grattafiori et al., 2024] (availability-triggered, recorded deviation), leg 2 DeepSeek-V4-Flash, a conditional capability-ladder re-entry adjudicated under the registered escalation rule, not independent validation; an access guard ensured evaluation labels were touched exactly once, by the final evaluation (both recorded deviations are logged in Appendix G.8).

Pre-deployment certificate output: honest refusal. The $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ certificate’s output in this audit is pre-deployment honest refusal, consuming no evaluation labels: 1,000/1,000 refusals per leg, 2,000/2,000 total, 0 violations, each carrying a risk-side axis attribution (Figure 6a).

Post-hoc frontier diagnosis, kept separate. Separately from the certificate’s output, a post-hoc evaluation-set frontier diagnosis prices the frontier at $\hat { \beta } ^ { * } ( . 1 0 ) = . 0 5 0 ( \log 1 )$ and .209 (leg 2), both far below the floor $\beta = . 6 0$ , which lies outside both bootstrap intervals. Sweeping the risk level $\alpha \in \{ . 0 5 , . \bar { 1 } 0 , . 1 5 , . 2 0 \}$ traces the full frontier-vs-floor map (Figure 6a): the floor $\beta = . 6 0$ is uncrossed (and outside every bootstrap interval) at every tested α for both legs (even the stronger leg 2 reaches only $\hat { \beta } ^ { * } ( . 2 0 ) = . 5 2 5 , \mathrm { C I } \left[ . 4 6 4 , . 5 9 7 \right] )$ . So the post-hoc evaluation-set frontier estimate stays below the floor across the tested operating plane, not only at the SLA point (per-α 500-resample bootstrap intervals; no simultaneous-coverage claim across the α-grid or over threshold selection, and the closest case is leg 2 at $\alpha = . 2 0$ , whose interval ends at .597), consistent with frontier-infeasibility across the tested plane rather than a population proof of it. Capability escalation is monotone for $\alpha \geq . 1 0$ (leg 2 above leg 1) but moves the frontier without ever crossing the floor. We keep the two outputs separate: the certificate refuses pre-deployment, while the frontier number is post-hoc evaluation-set diagnosis; the certificate does not prove the frontier, and we do not claim it does.

Plug-in contrast, leg-split only. The plug-in baseline fails leg-specifically, and we report it leg-split only (Figure 6b): leg 1 claims 99.9%, violates 99.9%, risk $. 2 7 8 \ : = \ : 2 . 8 \times \alpha ;$ leg 2 claims 0.1%, and the lone claim violates, risk $. 2 1 9 = 2 . 2 \times \alpha$ at coverage .614. The two legs fail in opposite modes (silent violation at scale versus claim collapse): precisely the information a pooled summary would destroy.

Refusal robustness: status only (Claim C6). The leg-2 refusal is invariant over registered nuisance choices: $K \in \{ 8 , 1 6 , 3 2 \} \times B \in \{ 5 , 1 0 , 2 0 \}$ } plus lock-quantile jitter, 11/11 no-cert, with the risk side failing 64/64 lattice points in every variant (Table A1, Appendix G.6). This is refusal-status robustness only, not validation of the stratified-shift model: on the real workload misspecification-to-breakage was not reached, though a synthetic adversarial sweep exhibits the breakage boundary in $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ but off the $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ K-measurable class (Appendix G.9), and the nuisance necessity narrows to the unknown-η edge (§4.5, Theorem 8).

What an operator learns. Honest refusal is the pre-committed first-class outcome here: the attribution says which test failed; the separate post-hoc frontier diagnosis (§5.3) is what supports reading this SLA scenario as frontier-infeasible, and under that reading no n or m purchase helps (raise capability or renegotiate), while the leg gap prices exactly that.

## 6 Discussion

On the assumptions. The map rests on load-bearing assumptions. Bounded-ratio covariate shift $( w \leq B )$ is what makes the floor estimable label-free while leaving the risk unidentified in Model-B; the resulting inconsistency over $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ (Theorem 6) is precisely why the map is stated across models rather than as a single-model minimax law; the restriction that restores consistency (oracle weights in Model-A, a pre-registered K-cell partition in Model-B<sup>′</sup>) is the object of study, not a convenience. The regular frontier margin $( \kappa , s _ { 0 } )$ is generic (Lemma 1) and, in its lattice realization $\bar { \mathrm { L R } } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( \bar { s } )$ for the upper bounds (Definition 4), isolates the local regime $s \leq s _ { 0 } \land c _ { 0 } \beta$ in which the budget law is linear; the stratified shift partition K is a dial, not a disguise (Remark 17), with its cost priced explicitly through the histogram nuisance.

Conditional validity and misspecification. Model-B<sup>′</sup> validity (Theorem 2) is conditional on the pre-registered K-cell model: the same epistemic status as the covariate-shift assumption itself. When w is not K-measurable the guarantee degrades to its K-projection with an additive bias that covariates alone cannot estimate (Remark 16); a deliberately adversarial synthetic sweep maps this breakage boundary directly: a perturbation in $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ but, by construction, off the $\smash { \mathbf { B } ^ { \prime } \mathcal { K } }$ -measurable class, invisible to the certificate, that nonetheless drives true violations once the receding frontier crosses the locked operating point (Appendix G.9). The nuisance necessity is settled only on its K-free necessary core, with the remainder sharply localized: a K-free core is necessary for lattice-valued certification in the known-η submodel (and matched from below), the histogram $B ^ { 2 } K$ rate is proved sufficient but not proved necessary, and exactly one edge, the unknown-η case, remains open (Theorem 8).

Practical reading. The certificate’s first-class output is a certify-or-refuse decision carrying a deterministic, observable test-side attribution: a refusal says which test failed, and for the data-starved axes Corollary 1 reads off which additional sampling extends the guarantee, while a separate frontier diagnosis signals when no purchase helps and the finer nuisance-limited label requires $( \kappa , s )$ as planning values (§4.5). The practical constants are harsh and reported in full, as one slice of a characterized envelope rather than an isolated point: $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ certifies 8/1,024 audit cells against oracle-A’s 583/1,024, and at the audited slice needs 64× the oracle-A budget to fully certify (128× at the first-certification onset); across the registered K-sweep this onset premium scales as $K ^ { 0 . 9 5 5 }$ (95% CI [0.755, 1.154]). This premium is measured against the released two-family oracle kernel (Remark $1 0 ) .$ , which also differs from $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ in confidence allocation and floor construction, so it prices the whole estimated-weight construction and upper-bounds, rather than isolates, the histogram estimator’s $\ell ^ { 1 }$ weight-recovery cost (Proposition 1). The premium is not a proved necessary certification cost (the established necessary core is K-free for lattice-valued certification, unknown-η still open; Theorem $^ { 8 ) }$ . Nor is it specific to the empirical-Bernstein risk test: the equal-construction alternative we tested, a SCoRE-inspired floor-augmented betting arm, is valid by Proposition 5 and, in the matched-replication head-to-head, weakly dominates $\mathbf { B ^ { \prime } }$ in observed certify-frequency without enlarging the powered set while sharing its conservatism (an ordering observed in that run, with no paired test reported; it is a risk-test ablation rather than a source-faithful SCoRE implementation, §5.2). The certificate’s contribution is therefore the lower-bound theory, the two-resource map, and the explicitly priced nuisance, not a finite-sample power advantage.

Limitations. (i) Two-axis support: at the registered 41-cell density the CI-separation criterion failed, but it is recovered at higher design density (unanimous strict non-overlap by 635 cells, with the additive form 2.2× better than the nearest alternative), confirming the registered sample-limited reading. The per-axis exponent-containment sub-test remains the finer residual check (Appendix G.9). (ii) Contour constants do not transfer across shift intensities (rate-shape and ordering only; the drift is systematic and monotone in intensity, Appendix G.9). (iii) Localized-geometry evidence is restricted to $\mathbf { \bar { B } ^ { \prime } }$ family 1 plus qualified family-2 within-pair structure. (iv) B<sup>′</sup> validity is conditional on the pre-registered shift model and off-partition robustness is not claimed: the real workload shows refusal-status invariance only, and the synthetic breakage sweep above (Appendix G.9) is a sensitivity characterization, not a guarantee. (v) The real-workload scope is a single-corpus domain-shift audit with no positive certification, with the post-hoc evaluation-set frontier estimate below the floor across the whole tested operating plane $( \alpha \leq . 2 0$ , both legs; per-α bootstrap, not a population proof). (vi) The implementable certificate is far more sample-hungry than oracle weights: those practical constants (reported in full above) are harsh but fully characterized, one slice of an envelope rather than an isolated point (Appendix G.9).

## 7 Conclusion

A hard coverage floor is a statistical object in its own right: it makes the feasibility frontier operational, splits certification cost, in the model-split sense of Theorem 1, into labeled-source and unlabeled-target resources, and surfaces a localized accepted-region functional (not global ESS) that drives the upper-bound variance proxy and organizes the lower-bound hard-slice geometry. This correspondence rests on the matched bound structure and the family-1 experiments, and a fixed ESS separation theorem is left open. Certification with honest refusal is implementable under a pre-registered stratifiedshift model: the formal-guarantee arms are valid by theorem, with 0 violations across the 591 cells they certify on the synthetic grid; on the real workload its output is honest refusal (2,000/2,000), a feasibility verdict, not validity evidence.

Outlook. The pre-registered empirical program (Appendix G.10) is largely complete, and two directions remain. First, beyond the clean B<sup>′</sup> family-1 evidence, the localized-geometry comparison on family 2 remains censored; an uncensored family-2 rerun with a fixed-range-constant oracle test would test whether the localized accepted-region functional governs the rate beyond B<sup>′</sup> family 1. Second, the nuisance theory is settled only on one side: the K-free necessary core is proved for lattice-valued certification in the known-η submodel, and no lower bound forces a separate source-covariate split for estimating p at the population level (Theorem 8); whether unknown-η Model-B<sup>′</sup> admits a K-free certificate, or instead forces K back through the unknown-η source↔target alignment, is the central open edge.
