## A Frontier Characterization and Regular-Margin Lemmas

This appendix proves the frontier-level statements behind $\ S 3 { \mathrm { : } }$ the genericity of the regular margin, the linear budget law, the population frontier characterization, and the lattice bookkeeping packaged by $\mathrm { L } \mathrm { \bar { R } } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } }$

Notation bridge. The main text writes $\beta ^ { * } ( \alpha , Q )$ for the feasibility frontier defined through the budget functional in $\ S 3 ;$ this is the relaxed (fractional-knapsack) frontier: the supremum of $C _ { Q }$ over all randomized acceptance rules $S : \mathcal { X }  [ 0 , 1 ]$ satisfying the ratio constraint; maximizing the linear functiona $\mathbf { \dot { C } } _ { Q } = \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ S ]$ subject to the single linear budget constraint $\mathbb { E } _ { Q } \big [ S ( \bar { \eta } - \alpha ) \big ] \leq 0$ is a generalized Neyman–Pearson problem, solved by the increasing-rearrangement threshold rule [Dantzig and Wald, 1951]. It coincides with the supremum over deterministic $\{ 0 , 1 \}$ selectors when $Q _ { X }$ is nonatomic on the score level sets (no integrality gap); when $\bar { Q } _ { X }$ has atoms straddling the frontier the deterministic selector frontier can be strictly smaller, since a fractional sliver of an atom is not deterministically attainable (the sharp-threshold construction of Proposition 2 is exactly such an atomic instance). This relaxation gap never affects the achievability theorems: their coverage slack $s = \beta ^ { * } - \stackrel { \cdot } { \beta }$ is measured against the relaxed frontier $\beta ^ { * }$ , but the certificate uses only the margin-bearing lattice witness $\lambda _ { s }$ (never the equality $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } = \beta ^ { * } )$ , so a residual $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } \le \beta ^ { * }$ gap is harmless; the witness-only role of $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } }$ is spelled out below. For a fixed lattice Λ the operative object is the latticefrontier

$$
\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } ( \alpha , Q ) = \operatorname* { s u p } \{ C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) : \lambda \in \Lambda , \ R _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \leq \alpha \} ( \operatorname * { s u p } \varnothing : = 0 ) ,
$$

and $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } \le \beta ^ { * }$ always. Equality, up to grid resolution, is a lattice-design property requiring two conditions: the threshold family lies on a score whose order agrees with η on the relevant range, and its coverage grid is both sufficiently fine and positioned to bracket the operative frontier neighborhood. Quantitatively, if the coverage mesh $h _ { C }$ (the largest gap between attainable lattice coverages) satisfies $h _ { C } < s / 8$ and some attainable coverage lies in $\left[ \beta ^ { * } - h _ { C } , \beta ^ { * } \right]$ (the grid reaches the frontier, not merely the interior neighborhood), then $0 \leq \beta ^ { * } - \beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } \leq h _ { C } ;$ a fine mesh that brackets only $\beta ^ { * } - s / 2$ controls the witness (below) but not $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * }$ itself; a grid whose attainable coverages stop short of $\beta ^ { * }$ (say at $\beta ^ { * } - 2 h _ { C } )$ leaves $\beta ^ { * } - \beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } \ge 2 h _ { C }$ despite $h _ { C } < s / 8$ . The power theorems invoke $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s )$ only as the explicit existence of a margin-bearing lattice witness $\lambda _ { s } ;$ the condition alone does not imply $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } = \beta ^ { * }$ , and a coarse lattice can satisfy it while still having $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } < \beta ^ { * } \left( \mathrm { e . g } \right.$ . a single in-window witness at coverage $\beta + s / 4$ with the next feasible point far below $\beta ^ { * } )$ . We keep $\beta ^ { * }$ (relaxed) and $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * }$ (lattice) notationally distinct, write $\Delta _ { \Lambda } \overset { \cdot } { : } = \beta ^ { * } - \beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } \geq 0$ for the discretization gap $( \mathrm { s o } 0 \leq \Delta _ { \Lambda } \leq h _ { C }$ under the bracketing condition above, and $\Delta _ { \Lambda } = 0$ up to grid resolution when both lattice-design conditions hold), and write $s = \beta ^ { * } - \beta$ for the relaxed coverage slack in the power statements; in any construction that needs the lattice slack (notably the inconsistency lattice of Theorem 6), we verify the lattice realizes the relevant relaxed frontier explicitly $( \mathrm { i } . { \mathsf { e } } . \Delta _ { \Lambda }$ is controlled).

## A.1 Genericity of the regular margin

Lemma 1 (Genericity). Suppose $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( u ) <$ α on a set ofpositive measure and $\beta ^ { * } < 1$ . Then $\varphi$ is strictly positive on an interval $\left( 0 , c _ { \alpha } \right]$ (where $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } < \alpha ) ,$ , strictly decreasing wherever $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } > \alpha _ { : }$ , and at the zero-crossing $\beta ^ { * }$ the essential left limit satisfies $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( \beta ^ { * } ) > \alpha$ strictly (equivalently $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } \ge \alpha + \kappa$ a.e. on a left-neighborhood of $\beta ^ { * } )$ , unless $\varphi \equiv 0$ on an interval (a degenerate tie $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } \equiv \alpha )$ . Hence the regularfrontier margin holds except in the degenerate boundary case.

Proof. $\varphi ^ { \prime } ( c ) = \alpha - \eta _ { O } ^ { * } ( c )$ for ${ \mathrm { { a . e . ~ } } } c .$ By assumption $\begin{array} { r } { \varphi ( c _ { \alpha } ) = \int _ { 0 } ^ { c _ { \alpha } } \bigl ( \alpha - \eta _ { Q } ^ { * } \bigr ) d u > 0 } \end{array}$ for $c _ { \alpha }$ the supremum of the region where $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } < \alpha$ , and $\beta ^ { * } > c _ { \alpha }$ . Since $\varphi ( { \beta } ^ { * } ) = 0$ with $\varphi ( c _ { \alpha } ) > 0$ , the derivative must be negative somewhere on $( c _ { \alpha } , \beta ^ { * } )$ i.e. $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( c ) > \alpha$ for some c in that interval; monotonicity of the rearrangement then gives $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( u ) \geq \eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( c ) > \alpha$ for a.e. $u \in ( c , \beta ^ { * } )$ . Since $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * }$ is defined only up to a.e. equivalence, we read the crossing through this a.e. left-neighborhood margin (equivalently the essential left limit $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( \beta ^ { * } ^ { - } ) ,$ , not a single point value. Strictness at the crossing fails only if $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } = \alpha$ on a neighborhood of $\beta ^ { * }$ , which is the excluded degenerate tie. Concretely, fixing such a $c \in ( c _ { \alpha } , \beta ^ { * } )$ with $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( c ) > \alpha$ and setting $\kappa : = \eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( c ) - \alpha > 0$ and $s _ { 0 } : = \beta ^ { * } - c > 0$ , monotonicity of $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * }$ gives $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( u ) \geq \eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( c ) = \alpha + \kappa$ for a.e. $u \in [ \beta ^ { * } - s _ { 0 } , \beta ^ { * } )$ : an explicit regular margin $( \kappa , s _ { 0 } )$ , obtained from the rearrangement’s monotonicity alone (no continuity of $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * }$ at $\beta ^ { * }$ is needed). □

## A.2 The linear budget law

For a world with regular margin $( \kappa , s _ { 0 } )$ and $0 \leq s \leq s _ { 0 }$

$$
\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ) = \varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ) - \varphi ( \beta ^ { * } ) = \int _ { \beta ^ { * } - s } ^ { \beta ^ { * } } \left( \eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( u ) - \alpha \right) d u \geq \kappa s ,\tag{2}
$$

using $\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } ) = 0$ and $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } \ge \alpha + \kappa \mathrm { a . e }$ . on the integration range; this is the linear budget law quoted in §3. The matching upper bound is $\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ) \leq \bar { K } s$ with $\bar { K } = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { u < \beta ^ { * } } ( \eta _ { O } ^ { * } ( u ) - \alpha ) _ { + } \leq 1 - \alpha$ . The risk budget available at coverage slack s is therefore linear in s: the single fact that fixes the exponent −2 on the labeled axis (Appendix B) and supplies the achievability margins (Appendix C). The endpoint-only condition $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( \beta ^ { * } ) - \alpha \ge \kappa$ would not suffice: monotonicity of $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * }$ runs the wrong way, so the left-neighborhood form in the definition is load-bearing.

## A.3 The margin-bearing lattice assumption

The assumption $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s )$ used by Theorems 1, 3, and 7 reads: there exists $\lambda _ { s } \in \Lambda$ with

$$
C _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { s } ) \in [ \beta + s / 4 , \beta ^ { * } - s / 4 ] \qquad \mathrm { a n d } \qquad - G _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { s } ) \ = \ \mathbb { E } _ { Q } \left[ S _ { \lambda _ { s } } ( \alpha - \eta ) \right] \ \ge \ \kappa s / 8 .
$$

A coverage-only form (∃λ with the right coverage and $R _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \leq \alpha )$ is not sufficient for the power proofs: an arbitrary lattice point at that coverage may have risk budget arbitrarily close to 0, because the budget law (2) bounds the rearranged-optimal set’s budget, not an arbitrary λ’s. $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s )$ holds automatically for risk-score-aligned nestedthreshold lattices under a regular margin $( \kappa , s _ { 0 } )$ with $s \leq s _ { 0 } ,$ , whose grid is finer than $s / 8$ and whose attainable coverages bracket $\beta ^ { * } - s / 2$ on both sides, namely thresholds on a score whose order agrees with η on the relevant range (so each accepting set is a risk sublevel set $\{ \eta < t _ { \lambda } \}$ , equivalently a rearranged-optimal set), because there the lattice’s acceptance sets are the rearranged-optimal sets and inherit the budget φ exactly. Concretely, the aligned threshold whose coverage is nearest $\beta ^ { * } - { \mathfrak { s } } / 2$ lands in the window $[ \beta ^ { * } - 3 s / \bar { 4 } , \dot { \beta } ^ { * } - s / \bar { 4 } ] = [ \beta + s / 4 , \beta ^ { * } - \overline { { s } } / 4 ]$ (width $s / 2 .$ , which exceeds the $< s / 8$ coverage mesh, so once the aligned lattice’s coverage range brackets $\beta ^ { * } - s / 2$ which is automatic for a grid spanning the operative frontier neighborhood, such a threshold exists); writing its slack $s ^ { \prime } = \beta ^ { * } - C _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { s } ) \in [ s / \bar { 4 } , 3 s / 4 ] \subseteq [ \bar { 0 } , s _ { 0 } ]$ , the budget law $( 2 ) \mathrm { g i v e s } - G _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { s } ) = \varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ^ { \prime } ) \ge \kappa s ^ { \prime } \ge \kappa s / \bar { 4 } \ge \kappa s / 8 ,$ so both clauses of $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } } ( s )$ hold with room to spare. For misaligned scores the shortfall is precisely a representation gap, and certification can fail for lattice-design reasons rather than statistical ones.

## A.4 Frontier characterization

Theorem 4 (Frontier characterization). Fix a world $( P , Q _ { X } , \eta ) \in \mathcal { W } _ { B } , \alpha \in ( 0 , 1 )$ , and $\beta > 0 .$

(a) $H \beta > \beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } ( \alpha , Q )$ , then every $\lambda \in \Lambda$ violates $R _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \leq \alpha o r C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \geq \beta ;$ consequently any $( \alpha , \beta , \delta )$ -valid procedure satisfies $\mathbb { P } ( \mathcal { A } \neq \bot ) \le \delta$ in that world.

(b) $I f 0 < \beta \leq \beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } ( \alpha , Q )$ and the supremum is attained by some λ with $C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) > 0 ;$ , at least one certifiable λ exists.

The hypothesis $\beta > 0$ matches the certify convention of§3: a coverage-0 policy has $R _ { Q } = + \infty$ and is never feasible, so it is excludedfrom the supremum defining $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * }$ , and part (b)’s positive-coverage attainment hypothesis already covers the $\beta = 0$ boundary.

Proof. (a) Take any λ with $C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \geq \beta ;$ since $\beta > 0$ this excludes the $C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) = 0$ convention case. Then $C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \geq$ $\beta > \beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * }$ implies λ is not in the feasible set $\{ R _ { Q } \leq \alpha \}$ , by definition of the supremum; hence $R _ { Q } ( \lambda ) > \alpha$ . So every certified output is incorrect in this world, and validity caps the certification probability at δ. (b) Immediate from the definitions. □

Remark 3 (Population vs. statistical impossibility). Theorem 4(a) is population-level: above the frontier there is nothing to certify. The statistical impossibility (that near the frontier no procedure can know which side it is on) is Theorem 5, and the two are deliberately separated.

Remark 4 (Discrete lattices break the linear map within cells). Between adjacent lattice coverages the certifiable slack is piecewise constant, so the budget available to a fixed lattice cell does not scale linearly in s within the cell: positive-budget cells behave as plateaus, and zero-margin cells drive the required n to infinity. This is a lattice-design diagnostic rather than a pathology of the map; it generates the constant-budget plateau lower bound of Lemma 3 (Appendix D) and is observed empirically (Appendix G).

## B The Two-Axis Lower Bound

This appendix proves the formal version of Theorem 1, Claim 1: the Model-B two-axis lower bound, each axis witnessed by a Le Cam two-point pair at bounded ratio, and the additive combination obtained inside a single construction family per $( n , m )$ as two simultaneously witnessed necessary conditions (max form; the additive display follows up to a factor of 2, Appendix B.3), not a single joint KL summation.

Theorem 5 (Two-axis minimax lower bound, Model-B). There exist absolute constants $c _ { 1 } , c _ { 2 } > 0$ and,for each $B \geq 2$ $\alpha \in [ 0 . 2 , 0 . 9 ] , \beta \in ( 0 , 0 . 6 ]$ , a family of worlds in $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ with regular margin $\left( \kappa , s _ { 0 } \right)$ , κ ${ \mathrm { ; } } \in ( 0 , { \frac { 1 } { \cdot } }$ min{α, 1 − α}) (the $\kappa < \alpha / 2$ bound makes the margin $s _ { 0 } > 0$ , so thefamily is non-vacuous; the $\kappa \leq ( 1 - \alpha ) / 2$ bound keeps the Bernoulli levels in range), Bernoulli conditional losses $L \mid X \sim \operatorname { B e r n } ( \eta ( X ) )$ , and (for each s in the range below, chosen after s), a lattice Λ given by afinite nested grid ofscore thresholds with meshfiner than $s / 4$ containing the construction’s block boundaries, such that thefollowing holds in Model-B. Let $s \in ( 0 , s _ { 0 } ]$ with additionally $s \leq c _ { 0 } \beta$ and $\beta + s \le 1$ (the local-regime and valid-coverage conditions; for this family $c _ { 0 } = s _ { 0 } / \beta$ depends on $( \alpha , \kappa )$ , so $s \leq c _ { 0 } \beta$ is already implied by $s \leq s _ { 0 }$ and is displayed only to match the standing local-regime form of $\cdot \ S 3 )$ , and suppose a procedure A is $( \alpha , \beta , \delta )$ -valid on $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ with ${ \dot { \delta } } \leq 1 / 8 .$ . If

$$
s \ \leq \ c _ { 1 } { \frac { 1 } { \kappa } } { \sqrt { \frac { \beta } { n } } } \quad ( n { - } a x i s ) \qquad o r \qquad s \ \leq \ c _ { 2 } { \sqrt { \frac { \beta } { m } } } \quad ( m { - } a x i s ) ,
$$

then there is a world with coverage slack $\geq s$ on which $\mathbb { P } ( \mathcal { A } = \bot ) \ge 1 / 2 ;$ infact the slack is exactly sfor the displayed constructionfamilies, whose relaxedfrontier is $\beta ^ { * } = \beta + s ( A$ ppendices $B . l , B . 2 ) ,$ , so a certifiable λ exists with room to spare. Consequently the minimax no-cert window has width at least the maximum ofthe two expressions (hence at least half their sum), and certifying at slack s requires both $\iota \gtrsim \beta / ( \kappa s ) ^ { 2 }$ and m $\gtrsim \beta / s ^ { 2 }$

The $\sqrt { \beta }$ factor on the m-axis is intrinsic to the construction family (the perturbable safe block has mass $\ l \asymp \beta ) ;$ it makes both axes vanish as $\beta  0$ (Remark 7) and exactly matches the variance-aware floor rate $\sqrt { \beta ( 1 - \beta ) / m }$ on the achievability side (Theorem 7).

## B.3 Additive combination inside one construction family

A single procedure facing the class must survive both pairs; both pairs share the base world $W _ { 1 }$ , so the family $\{ W _ { 1 } , W _ { 2 } ^ { ( n ) } , W _ { 2 } ^ { ( m ) } \}$ , indexed by $( n , m )$ through the choices of $\Delta$ and ε, witnesses both axes simultaneously. The two bounds hold simultaneously, so the no-cert window is at least the maximum of the two axis widths, hence at least half their sum:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { s \ \leq \ \frac { 1 } { 2 } \Big ( c _ { 1 } \kappa ^ { - 1 } \sqrt { \beta / n } + c _ { 2 } \sqrt { \beta / m } \Big ) \ \Longrightarrow \ s \leq \operatorname* { m a x } \Big ( c _ { 1 } \kappa ^ { - 1 } \sqrt { \beta / n } , \ c _ { 2 } \sqrt { \beta / m } \Big ) } \\ { \implies \mathbb { P } _ { W _ { 1 } } ( A = \perp ) \geq \frac { 1 } { 2 } , } \end{array}
$$

which is the additive form of Theorem 1, Claim 1. Each axis also holds separately on the general class, since each pair lies in $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ with $w \in [ 1 / B , B ]$ bounded by construction and Q-support contained in P-support everywhere: nothing here touches positivity.

Remark 5 (The two main axes match within Model-B<sup>′</sup>). Both lower-bound families are weight-simple: the n-axis pair has w ≡ 1 (Appendix B.1) and the m-axis pair has w piecewise-constant on the fixed three-piece partition $\{ G , [ g , \dot { g ^ { \prime } } ) , H ^ { \prime } \}$ (Appendix B.2). Hence for any pre-registered partition K refining these pieces every world here is K-measurable and so lies in the sample-independent Model-B<sup>′</sup> class; a nested-threshold lattice cutting at cell boundaries gives union-of-cell acceptance, and Assumption $1 \mathrm { ^ s p } _ { \mathrm { m i n } }$ bound (so K may not be arbitrarily fine) is the certificate’s upper-bound feasibility condition, separate from class membership. Knowing K does not defeat the pairs: the n-axis pair differs only in the labels and the m-axis pair only in $Q _ { X }$ , so a Model-B<sup>′</sup> procedure’s per-cell statistics are measurable functions of the same $( n , m )$ draws and inherit the same $\mathrm { T V } \leq 1 / 4$ bound below $\mathbf { \bar { \rho } } n \asymp \beta / ( \kappa s ) ^ { 2 }$ and $m \times \beta / s ^ { 2 }$ . Therefore, on the two main axes the lower bound holds within Model-B<sup>′</sup> (not only cross-model): combined with the achievability of Theorem 3, under its standing hypotheses $\mathrm { ( L R _ { m a r g i n } }$ , a regular margin $( \kappa , s _ { 0 } )$ with s in the local regime, and the nuisance-budget condition $\rho _ { \lambda _ { s } } \leq \kappa s / 3 2 )$ , the risk and floor axes are same-model rate-matched up to constants and logarithms over the compatible-K subclass as totals $( n , m )$ (a pointwise, instance-dependent total-rate correspondence, not a uniform minimax over the class), in the regime where the weight splits do not dominate (so $n \asymp n _ { r } , m \asymp m _ { f } ;$ Corollary 2); on the algorithm’s test sub-block $( n _ { r } , m _ { f } )$ alone this is a rate correspondence, not a sub-block lower bound, since a $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ procedure may reuse the weight-split draws for testing. The lone unmatched axis is the histogram nuisance $B ^ { 2 } K$ , whose necessity in the unknown-η case is the single open edge (Theorem 8, Appendix H). This narrows the cross-model gap of Theorem 1 to the nuisance axis (on the totals, in that regime), not the two resource axes.

## B.4 No matching Model-B upper bound: inconsistency over $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$

Theorem 5 lower-bounds Model-B at the same two-axis rate that the oracle-weight certificate of Theorem 7 attains, so the rate matches across the models. It is natural to ask whether the match is also within Model-B: is there an unknown-weight procedure (knowing only $w \leq B$ , estimating everything from n source labels and m unlabeled target covariates) that certifies at the Model-A rate uniformly over $\dot { \mathcal { W } } _ { B } ?$ Theorem 6 answers in the negative, and decisively: over the full bounded-ratio class, Model-B is not merely rate-separated but inconsistent at the operating point $\alpha = \beta = 1 / 2 ;$ its minimax certifiable slack there does not vanish at any sample size. This is why the implementable certificate of $\ S 3$ restricts to a pre-registered finite shift model (Model-B<sup>′</sup>): a complexity restriction on the weights is necessary for consistency, not a convenience.

Theorem 6 (Model-B inconsistency). Fix $\alpha = \beta = 1 / 2 , B \geq 2 , \gamma = 1 / 2 .$ . There are an absolute constant $s _ { * } = 1 / 4$ and two nested thresholds, λ<sub>τ</sub> accepting $[ 0 , 9 / 1 6 ]$ and λ<sub>t</sub>⋆ accepting $[ 0 , 3 / 4 ]$ , both independent ofb, n, and m, such thatfor every $b \in ( 0 , 1 / 4 ]$ , every pair $( n , m )$ ofsample sizes, and everyfinite nested-threshold lattice $\Lambda \supseteq \left\{ \lambda _ { \tau } , \lambda _ { t ^ { \star } } \right\}$ there exist two priors $\pi ^ { + } , \pi ^ { - }$ supported on worlds of $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ with regular margin $( \kappa , s _ { \mathrm { r e g } } ) , \kappa = b ,$ such that: every world in $\operatorname { s u p p } ( \pi ^ { - } )$ has frontier slack $\geq s _ { * }$ and admits a certifiable lattice point (coverage $\geq \beta ,$ risk margin $\geq b \gamma / 2 ) ;$ in every world ofsupp(π<sup>+</sup>) every lattice point with coverage $\ge \beta$ is risk-infeasible; and the laws ofthe observed data $( D _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { s r c } } , U _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { t g t } } )$ under the two priors satisfy $\mathrm { T V } \leq 1 / 4 .$ . Consequently no $( \alpha , \beta , \delta )$ -valid Model-B procedure with $\delta \leq 1 / 8$ certifies thefeasible $\pi ^ { - }$ worlds with probab $i l i t y > 1 / 2$ . Hence on any such lattice, fixed and pre-registered in advance, no valid Model-B procedure certifies allfrontier-slack-s<sub>∗</sub> worlds of $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ at any $( n , m )$ : Model-B is inconsistent over $\mathcal { W } _ { B } ,$ , in contrast to Model-A (Theorem 7) and Model-B<sup>′</sup> (Theorem 3).

Construction. Let $\gamma = 1 / 2 , g = \beta / 2 = 1 / 4 , \zeta = 1 / 1 6 , \tau = g + h$ with $h = g + \zeta = 5 / 1 6$ the mass of the gadget band, and a deep-safe margin $d = b \gamma / 4$ . Labels are Bernoulli, L | X ∼ Bern $. ( \eta ( X ) )$ as in Appendix B.1. Take $\check { K }$ even (chosen below), cell mass $p = h / K$ with $p \leq \gamma g / ( 4 ( 1 + \gamma ^ { 2 } ) )$ . On $\mathcal { X } = [ 0 , 1 ]$ with $P _ { X } = \mathrm { U n i f } \mathrm { : }$

$G = [ 0 , g ) \colon w \equiv 1 , \eta = \alpha - d$ (a deep-safe block that forces the floor; its margin $d < b \gamma$ is deliberately smaller than the gadget amplitude: this is the crux of global infeasibility, and is where this construction departs from the surplus-anchored $\mathsf { \bar { W } } _ { 1 }$ of Appendix B.1).

$\Phi = [ g , \tau ) ;$ : partitioned into $K$ cells of mass $p$ grouped into $K / 2$ adjacent pairs. Per pair r draw $Z _ { r } \in \{ \pm 1 \}$ i.i.d. uniform; the two cells of pair r carry loss-signs $v = ( Z _ { r } , - Z _ { r } ) , \mathrm { i . e . } \ \eta = \alpha + b v$ , and weight-signs u with $w = 1 + \gamma u ( \mathrm { s o } w \in \{ 1 / 2 , 3 / 2 \} \subset [ 1 / \bar { B } , B ]$ for $B \geq 2 )$ . Under $\pi ^ { + }$ set $u = v ;$ under $\pi ^ { - }$ set $u = - v$ . Pairing forces $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { i \in \Phi } u _ { i } = \sum _ { i \in \Phi } v _ { i } = \stackrel { \cdot } { 0 } } \end{array}$ exactly.

• H = [τ, 1]: w ≡ 1, η = α + b (hot).

Thresholds are the nested policies $\lambda _ { t } = \{ \mathrm { a c c e p t } [ 0 , t ] \}$ ; the theorem fixes two of them, the Φ–H boundary $\lambda _ { \tau }$ and the hot-block frontier threshold $\lambda _ { t ^ { \star } }$ of (R2), and any lattice containing this pair may be used (R4). Because the per-pair weight-signs sum to zero, the Q-mass of each pair equals its P-mass 2p in both priors; hence $C _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { \tau } ) = g + h = \beta + \zeta$ identically, and the pair-occupancy law is the same under $\pi ^ { + }$ and $\pi ^ { - }$

(R1) Lattice infeasibility under $\pi ^ { + }$ . For one aligned pair (cells $( \eta , w ) = ( \alpha + b , 1 + \gamma )$ and $( \alpha - b , 1 - \gamma )$ , each of $P { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { m a s s } } p )$ , the Q-budget contribution is

$$
p ( 1 + \gamma ) { \big ( } \alpha - ( \alpha + b ) { \big ) } + p ( 1 - \gamma ) { \big ( } \alpha - ( \alpha - b ) { \big ) } = p b { \big [ } - ( 1 + \gamma ) + ( 1 - \gamma ) { \big ] } = - 2 p b \gamma = - b \gamma \cdot { \mathrm { ( p a i r ~ m a s ) } } .
$$

Consider any threshold t with $C _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { t } ) \geq \beta$ . Thresholds inside G have coverage at most $g = \beta / 2 < \beta$ , so t reaches into Φ; write its accepted Φ-part as matched pair portions of total P-mass ℓ plus one residual cell portion of mass $\leq p$ (worst case a low-weight safe cell, contributing $\leq b ( 1 - \gamma ) p$ to the budget). Cutting inside a pair is covered: if the two cells of the active pair contribute accepted masses a and c, the matched portions of mass min $( a , c )$ each enter ℓ and the residue $| a - c | \leq p$ is the residual portion. Coverage $\ge \beta = 2 g$ forces the accepted Φ $Q { \cdot } \mathrm { m a s s } \geq g .$ , and since the partial cell adds Q-mass $\leq ( 1 + \gamma ) p$ , we have $\ell + ( 1 + \gamma ) p \geq g$ . Therefore

$$
\varphi _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { t } ) \ \leq \ \underbrace { d g } _ { \mathcal { G } } - b \gamma \ell \ + \ b ( 1 - \gamma ) p \ \leq \ ( d - b \gamma ) g + b ( 1 + \gamma ^ { 2 } ) p \ \leq \ - \frac { 1 } { 2 } b \gamma g \ < \ 0 ,
$$

using $d = b \gamma / 4$ and $p \leq \gamma g / ( 4 ( 1 + \gamma ^ { 2 } ) )$ . At $t = \tau , \varphi _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { \tau } ) = d g - b \gamma h < 0$ , and accepting into H only subtracts more. So under $\pi ^ { + }$ every lattice point with coverage $\geq \beta$ has $R _ { Q } > \alpha ;$ any non-refusal is therefore invalid, and validity forces $\mathbb { P } ( \mathcal { A } \neq \bot ) \le \delta$

(R2) Feasibility with constant slack under $\pi ^ { - }$ . For one anti-aligned pair (cells $( \alpha + b , 1 - \gamma )$ and $( \alpha - b , 1 + \gamma ) )$ the Q-budget contribution is +2pbγ. Hence at $\lambda _ { \tau }$ (accept $G \cup \Phi ) , { \bar { C } } _ { Q } ( \bar { \lambda _ { \tau } } ) = \beta + \dot { \zeta } \geq \beta$ and

$$
\varphi _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { \tau } ) = d g + b \gamma h > 0 , \qquad \mathrm { s o } \qquad \alpha - R _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { \tau } ) = \frac { \varphi _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { \tau } ) } { \beta + \zeta } = \frac { b \gamma ( h + g / 4 ) } { 2 g + \zeta } \ \geq \ \frac { 1 } { 2 } b \gamma ,
$$

a constant risk margin (numerically $b / 3 )$ . The frontier sits at $\beta ^ { * } = g + ( 1 + \gamma ) h + ( d / b ) g ,$ , so the frontier slack is $s _ { * } = \beta ^ { * } - \beta = \gamma g \bar { + } ( 1 + \gamma ) \zeta + ( \bar { d } / \dot { b } ) g = 1 / 4$ , a fixed positive constant independent of $b , n , m$ . Lattice realization of the frontier. The second threshold the theorem fixes is the hot-block threshold $\lambda _ { t ^ { \star } }$ at $t ^ { \star } = \beta ^ { * } = 3 / 4 $ since $w \equiv 1$ on $H , C _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { t ^ { \star } } ) = t ^ { \star } = \beta ^ { * }$ and $\begin{array} { r } { \varphi _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { t ^ { \star } } ) = \varphi _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { \tau } ) - b ( t ^ { \star } - \tau ) = \frac { 3 b } { 1 6 } - b \cdot \frac { 3 } { 1 6 } = 0 } \end{array}$ , i.e. $R _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { t ^ { \star } } ) = \alpha$ (feasible at equality). Hence $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } = \beta ^ { * } = 3 / 4$ on every admissible lattice, since $\lvert \beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * } \le \beta ^ { * }$ always, and $s _ { * } = 1 / 4$ is a genuine lattice-frontier slack, not merely the relaxed one. Requiring $\lambda _ { t ^ { \star } }$ is exactly what excludes a lattice leaving H uncut, on which $\beta _ { \Lambda } ^ { * }$ would instead collapse to the integer coverage $\beta + \zeta = \dot { 9 } / 1 6$ , a lattice-design artifact. Its presence leaves R1 and R3 untouched: under $\pi ^ { + }$ it accepts hot mass and is only more infeasible, and the data law depends on $( P , Q , \eta )$ , not on $\Lambda ;$ ; the displayed certifiable lattice point with strictly positive margin remains $\lambda _ { \tau }$ (coverage $9 / \bar { 1 6 }$ , margin $b / 3 \geq b \gamma / 2 )$ , the point certified in the Le Cam step. The interval just below the frontier lies in the hot level $\eta = \alpha + b $ , so the regular margin $( \kappa , s _ { \mathrm { r e g } } )$ holds with $\kappa = b$ . Thus the $\pi ^ { - }$ worlds are genuinely certifiable at constant slack. (Under $\pi ^ { + }$ the same hot left-neighborhood gives regular margin $\kappa = b$ as well; there the frontier sits at $\beta ^ { * } = g + ( 1 - \gamma ) h + ( d / b ) g = 7 / 1 6 < \beta ,$ so the world is infeasible, consistent with R1.)

(R3) Indistinguishability. G and H are identical under $\pi ^ { + }$ and $\pi ^ { - } \left( w \equiv 1 \right.$ , fixed $\eta )$ , contributing likelihood ratio 1; only $\Phi ^ { \prime } \mathbf { s }$ pairs carry the $\pi ^ { + } { \mathrm { - v e r s u s { - } } } \pi ^ { - }$ signal. The observations are: for each source draw, its cell and label; for each target draw, its cell. Given the pair assignment (which pair each point falls in, not the within-pair cell identity, which carries the $\pi ^ { + }  – \mathbf { v } \mathbf { s } – \pi ^ { - }$ signal) the prior factorizes over pairs (the $Z _ { r }$ are independent), and the pair-occupancy law is π-independent (R1 paragraph), so writing $N _ { r } , M _ { r }$ for the source and target occupancies of pair r and $\chi _ { r } ^ { 2 } \big ( N _ { r } , \mathbf { \bar { M } } _ { r } \big )$ for the conditional pairwise $\mathit { \check { \chi } } ^ { 2 }$ between the two priors,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { 1 + \chi ^ { 2 } ( P _ { + } \parallel P _ { - } ) = \mathbb { E } _ { \mathrm { a s s i g n } } \Big [ \prod _ { r } \left( 1 + \chi _ { r } ^ { 2 } ( N _ { r } , M _ { r } ) \right) \Big ] . } \end{array}\tag{3}
$$

Per-pair bound. The pairwise factor is controlled by the following self-contained lemma; the construction enters only through its hypotheses.

Lemma 2 (Pairwise correlation $\chi ^ { 2 } )$ . Fix $N , M \in \mathbb { N } _ { 0 }$ and $\theta , \gamma \in [ 0 , \frac { 1 } { 2 } ]$ , and let $Z \sim \mathrm { U n i f } \{ \pm 1 \}$ . Conditional on Z, let $X _ { 1 } , \dots , X _ { N } \in \{ \pm 1 \}$ be i.i.d. with $\mathbb { E } [ X _ { i } \mid Z ] = \theta Z _ { ; }$ , and independently let $Y _ { 1 } , . . . , Y _ { M } \overset { \cdot } { \in } \left\{ \pm 1 \right\}$ be i.i.d. with $\mathbb { E } [ Y _ { j } \mid Z ] = \sigma \gamma Z ,$ , where $\sigma \in \{ \pm 1 \}$ indexes the two laws $P _ { \sigma }$ of $( X _ { 1 : N } , Y _ { 1 : M } )$ (each marginalizing over $Z )$ . Thenfor an absolute constant $c _ { \chi } ,$

$$
1 + \chi ^ { 2 } ( P _ { + } \| P _ { - } ) \leq \exp \bigl ( c _ { \chi } \theta ^ { 2 } \gamma ^ { 2 } N M \bigr ) ,
$$

and $\chi ^ { 2 } ( P _ { + } \parallel P _ { - } ) = 0$ whenever $\theta = 0 o r \gamma = 0 .$

Proof. The signed sums $\begin{array} { r } { x = \sum _ { i } X _ { i } , y = \sum _ { j } Y _ { j } } \end{array}$ are sufficient for $Z .$ Let $q \pm , r \pm$ be the laws of $x , y$ given $Z = \pm 1$ and $q = { \textstyle \frac 1 2 } ( q _ { + } + q _ { - } ) , r = { \textstyle \frac 1 2 } ( r _ { + } + r _ { - } )$ the marginals; define posterior-mean coordinates $\begin{array} { r } { m ( x ) = \frac { q _ { + } ( x ) - q _ { - } ( x ) } { q _ { + } ( x ) + q _ { - } ( x ) } } \end{array}$ and $\begin{array} { r } { n ( y ) = \frac { r _ { + } ( y ) - r _ { - } ( y ) } { r _ { + } ( y ) + r _ { - } ( y ) } } \end{array}$ , both in $( - 1 , 1 )$ for $\theta , \gamma < 1$ , so $q _ { \pm } = q ( 1 \pm m )$ and $r _ { \pm } = r ( 1 \pm n )$ . Conditional independence of x, y given Z gives, under $P _ { \sigma }$

$$
g ^ { \sigma } ( x , y ) = { \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } } \sum _ { z = \pm 1 } q _ { z } ( x ) r _ { \sigma z } ( y ) = q ( x ) r ( y ) \bigl ( 1 + \sigma m ( x ) n ( y ) \bigr ) ,
$$

the linear-in-z term cancelling. Write $U = m ( x ) n ( y )$ under $q \otimes r ;$ then $| U | < 1$ , and $U \overset { d } { = } - U$ (the map $x \mapsto - x$ exchanges $q _ { + }  q _ { - }$ , flipping the sign of m). Hence

$$
\begin{array} { r } { 1 + \chi ^ { 2 } ( P _ { + } \| P _ { - } ) = \mathbb { E } _ { q \otimes r } \Big [ \frac { ( 1 + U ) ^ { 2 } } { 1 - U } \Big ] = \mathbb { E } \Big [ \frac { 1 + 3 U ^ { 2 } } { 1 - U ^ { 2 } } \Big ] = 1 + 4 \mathbb { E } \Big [ \frac { U ^ { 2 } } { 1 - U ^ { 2 } } \Big ] , } \end{array}
$$

the middle step by symmetrizing the integrand under $U \mapsto - U ;$ this vanishes when $\theta = 0 \mathrm { o r } \gamma = 0$ (then $m \equiv 0$ or $n \equiv 0 , { \mathrm { s o } } U \equiv 0 )$ . Two bounds on $\mathbb { E } [ U ^ { 2 } / ( 1 - U ^ { 2 } ) ]$ ] follow. Since $u \mapsto u / ( 1 - u )$ is increasing and $m ^ { 2 } n ^ { 2 } \leq m ^ { 2 }$ , the data-processing bound $\mathbb { E } [ U ^ { 2 } / ( 1 - U ^ { \smash { \prime } } ) ] \stackrel { \prime } { \leq } \mathbb { E } _ { q } [ m ^ { 2 } / \stackrel { \cdot } { ( } 1 - m ^ { 2 } ) ]$ ] holds (and symmetrically $\leq \mathbb { E } _ { r } [ n ^ { \overleftarrow { 2 } } / ( 1 - n ^ { 2 } ) ] ,$ . And from $\begin{array} { r }  \frac { \stackrel { \bar { m ^ { 2 } } \bar { n ^ { 2 } } } { 1 - \bar { m ^ { 2 } } \bar { n ^ { 2 } } } \leq \frac { \stackrel { \bar { m ^ { 2 } } } { 1 - \bar { m ^ { 2 } } } } { 1 - \bar { m ^ { 2 } } \bar { n ^ { 2 } } } \frac { \stackrel { \bar { n ^ { 2 } } } { 1 - \bar { n ^ { 2 } } } } { 1 - \bar { n ^ { 2 } } } } \end{array}$ (cross-multiplying, $( 1 - m ^ { 2 } ) ( 1 - n ^ { 2 } ) \leq 1 - m ^ { 2 } n ^ { 2 } )$ with independence, the product bound $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } [ U ^ { 2 } / ( 1 - U ^ { 2 } ) ] \le \mathbb { E } _ { q } [ \frac { m ^ { 2 } } { 1 - m ^ { 2 } } ] \mathbb { E } _ { r } [ \frac { n ^ { 2 } } { 1 - n ^ { 2 } } ] , } \end{array}$

The single-channel moment has a closed form. With $\begin{array} { r } { \psi = \left( \frac { 1 + \theta } { 1 - \theta } \right) ^ { x } } \end{array}$ one has $q _ { + } / q _ { - } = \psi$ , so $\textstyle m = { \frac { \psi - 1 } { \psi + 1 } }$ and ${ \frac { m ^ { 2 } } { 1 - m ^ { 2 } } } =$ $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { ( \psi - 1 ) ^ { 2 } } { 4 \psi } = \frac { 1 } { 4 } ( \psi - 2 + \psi ^ { - 1 } ) } \end{array}$ . Averaging over $\begin{array} { r } { q = \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( q _ { + } + q _ { - } ) } \end{array}$ and using the per-bit identity $\mathbb { E } _ { q _ { + } } [ \psi ^ { \pm 1 } ] \mathrm { : }$ under $q _ { + }$ $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } [ \psi ] = \left( \frac { 1 + 3 \theta ^ { 2 } } { 1 - \theta ^ { 2 } } \right) ^ { N } = : C _ { \theta } ^ { N } } \end{array}$ (each bit contributes $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 + \theta } { 2 } \frac { 1 + \theta } { 1 - \theta } + \frac { 1 - \theta } { 2 } \frac { 1 - \theta } { 1 + \theta } = \frac { 1 + 3 \theta ^ { 2 } } { 1 - \theta ^ { 2 } } ) } \end{array}$ while $\mathbb { E } _ { q _ { - } } [ \psi ] = 1$ , and symmetrically for $\psi ^ { - 1 }$ , gives

$$
\begin{array} { r } { { \mathbb { E } } _ { q } \Big [ \frac { m ^ { 2 } } { 1 - m ^ { 2 } } \Big ] = \frac { 1 } { 4 } \big ( C _ { \theta } ^ { N } - 1 \big ) , \qquad { \mathbb { E } } _ { r } \Big [ \frac { n ^ { 2 } } { 1 - n ^ { 2 } } \Big ] = \frac { 1 } { 4 } \big ( C _ { \gamma } ^ { M } - 1 \big ) , \quad C _ { \gamma } = \frac { 1 + 3 \gamma ^ { 2 } } { 1 - \gamma ^ { 2 } } . } \end{array}
$$

For $\theta , \gamma \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 }$ , log $\begin{array} { r } { C _ { \theta } \le C _ { \theta } - 1 = \frac { 4 \theta ^ { 2 } } { 1 - \theta ^ { 2 } } \le \frac { 1 6 } { 3 } \theta ^ { 2 } , } \end{array}$ , so $C _ { \theta } ^ { N } \leq e ^ { ( 1 6 / 3 ) \theta ^ { 2 } N }$ . Two regimes finish the proof. If $\theta ^ { 2 } N \leq 1$ and $\gamma ^ { 2 } M \leq 1$ , then $\begin{array} { r } { C _ { \theta } ^ { N } - 1 \le \frac { 1 6 } { 3 } \theta ^ { 2 } N e ^ { ( 1 6 / 3 ) \theta ^ { 2 } N } \le \frac { 1 6 } { 3 } e ^ { 1 6 / 3 } \theta ^ { 2 } N } \end{array}$ (using $e ^ { t } - 1 \leq t e ^ { t } )$ , and the product bound gives $\begin{array} { r } { \chi ^ { 2 } \le \frac { 1 } { 4 } ( \frac { 1 6 } { 3 } ) ^ { 2 } e ^ { 3 2 / 3 } \theta ^ { 2 } \gamma ^ { 2 } N M } \end{array}$ , whence $1 + \chi ^ { 2 } \leq \exp ( c \theta ^ { 2 } \gamma ^ { 2 } N M )$ . If instead $\theta ^ { 2 } N > 1$ , the data-processing bound gives $\begin{array} { r } { \dot { 1 } + \dot { \chi } ^ { 2 } \le C _ { \gamma } ^ { M } \le e ^ { ( 1 6 / 3 ) \gamma ^ { 2 } M } \le e ^ { ( 1 6 / 3 ) \theta ^ { 2 } \gamma ^ { 2 } N M } } \end{array}$ , and symmetrically $\mathrm { i f } \gamma ^ { 2 } M > 1$ . Taking $c _ { \chi }$ the larger of the two absolute constants proves the claim. □

Within a pair, conditional on $( N _ { r } , M _ { r } ) ;$ each source point falls in one of the pair’s two cells with an observed cell-sign $c \in \{ \pm 1 \}$ , and (loss-signs $\boldsymbol { v } \stackrel { \cdot } { = } ( Z _ { r } , - Z _ { r } )$ across the pair) its label has mean $\alpha + c b Z _ { r }$ . The cell-occupancy law is identical under $\pi ^ { + } , \pi ^ { - } \left( \mathbf { R } 1 \right.$ paragraph), so c is an ancillary, prior-independent label of the datum; the deterministic map $( c , L ) \mapsto ( c , X )$ with $X = \overset { \vartriangle } { \boldsymbol { c } } \left( 2 \bar { L } - \overset { \vartriangle } { \boldsymbol { 1 } } \right)$ is therefore a bijection of the observed pair that fixes $\chi ^ { 2 } ( P _ { + } | | P _ { - } )$ (the ancillary c has a prior-independent law and factors out), and at $\dot { \alpha } = 1 / 2$ the bit $X \in \{ \pm 1 \}$ has $\mathbb { E } [ X \mid ^ { \cdot } { \dot { Z } } _ { r } ] = { 2 } b { \dot { Z } } _ { \ i }$ in both cells. Thus the $N _ { r }$ source data reduce to i.i.d. bits of mean $\theta Z _ { r } , \dot { \theta } = 2 b$ . Symmetrically, each target point’s cell-identity within the pair is a {±1} bit whose bias is set by the weight-signs $u = \sigma v$ , of mean $\gamma \sigma Z _ { r } \left( \sigma = + 1 \right.$ under $\pi ^ { + } , \sigma = - 1$ under $\pi ^ { - } )$ . Lemma 2 applied with these $( \theta , \gamma , \dot { N } _ { r } , M _ { r } )$ then yields, for an absolute constant $c _ { \chi } ^ { \phantom { } } ;$

$$
1 + \chi _ { r } ^ { 2 } ( N _ { r } , M _ { r } ) \ \leq \ \exp \bigl ( c _ { \chi } b ^ { 2 } \gamma ^ { 2 } N _ { r } M _ { r } \bigr ) \qquad \mathrm { f o r ~ a l l ~ } N _ { r } , M _ { r } .\tag{4}
$$

Collision-count moment generatingfunction. Substituting (4) into (3) and writing $t = c _ { \chi } b ^ { 2 } \gamma ^ { 2 }$ and $\begin{array} { r } { W = \sum _ { r } N _ { r } M _ { r } } \end{array}$ (the source–target collision count over pairs),

$$
1 + \chi ^ { 2 } ( P _ { + } \parallel P _ { - } ) \ \leq \ \mathbb { E } \big [ e ^ { t W } \big ] .
$$

Because W is integer-valued with $0 \leq W \leq n m$ , and the source and target occupancies are independent with $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb E [ W ] = \sum _ { r } \mathbb E [ N _ { r } ] \overline { { \mathbb E [ M _ { r } ] } } = ( K / 2 ) ( n \cdot 2 h / K ) ( m \cdot 2 h / K ) = 2 h ^ { 2 } n m / K } \end{array}$

$$
\mathbb { E } \big [ e ^ { t W } \big ] \ \le \ 1 + ( e ^ { t n m } - 1 ) \mathbb { P } ( W \ge 1 ) \ \le \ 1 + ( e ^ { t n m } - 1 ) \mathbb { E } [ W ] \ = \ 1 + ( e ^ { t n m } - 1 ) \frac { 2 h ^ { 2 } n m } { K } .\tag{5}
$$

(The MGF is dominated by the rare high-collision events, so (5) is the honest bound; it is exponential in nm, but only the existence of a finite $K$ is needed.) Choosing any even

$$
K \ \ge \ K _ { * } ( n , m ) \ : = \ \operatorname* { m a x } \bigg \{ \frac { 4 h ( 1 + \gamma ^ { 2 } ) } { \gamma g } , \ \frac { \big ( e ^ { t n m } - 1 \big ) 2 h ^ { 2 } n m } { \log ( 5 / 4 ) } \bigg \}
$$

(finite for every n, m; the first term enforces the cell-size constraint $p \leq \gamma g / ( 4 ( 1 + \gamma ^ { 2 } ) ) )$ yields $\mathbb { E } [ e ^ { t W } ] \le 5 / 4$ , hence $\chi ^ { 2 } ( P _ { + } \| P _ { - } ) \leq 1 / 4$ and $\mathrm { T V } ( P _ { + } , P _ { - } ) \leq \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \sqrt { \chi ^ { 2 } } \leq 1 / 4$

(R4) The lattice is fixed, not adversarial. Neither the cell boundaries nor the cell count K enters R1–R3 through $\Lambda .$ R1 bounds $\varphi _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { t } )$ for every threshold t with $C _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { t } ) \geq \beta$ , so it applies verbatim to any nested-threshold lattice, however refined: additional thresholds only supply further points that R1 has already ruled infeasible under $\pi ^ { + }$ . R2 exhibits the certifiable point as $\lambda _ { \tau }$ (coverage $\bar { \beta + \zeta } = 9 / 1 6$ in both priors, each pair’s $Q$ -mass equalling its $P -$ mass, with risk margin $\geq b \gamma / 2 )$ and the frontier realization as $\lambda _ { t ^ { \star } }$ (coverage $t ^ { \star } = \beta ^ { * } = \mathbf { \bar { 3 } } / 4$ , since $w \equiv 1$ on $H ) ; \tau = g + h$ is the $\Phi { \mathrm { - } } H$ boundary and $t ^ { \star }$ lies inside $H$ , so both are absolute constants, unmoved by $K , n , \operatorname { o r } m$ . R3 is lattice-free by construction, the data law depending on $( P , Q , \eta )$ alone. The construction is therefore existential in the worlds, whose cell count $K _ { * } ( n , m )$ does grow with the sample sizes, as this construction requires, but not in the lattice, which may be fixed and pre-registered before any data is seen, as Definition 1 requires. In this respect Theorem 6 is stronger than Theorem 5, whose hard instance supplies its own compatible lattice per slack.

Le Cam conclusion. A valid procedure certifies with probability $\leq \delta \leq 1 / 8$ under every $\pi ^ { + }$ world (R1), hence under the $\pi ^ { + }$ data-mixture. If it certified each feasible π<sup>−</sup> world with probability $> 1 / 2$ it would certify under the $\pi ^ { - }$ mixture with probability $> 1 / 2 ;$ since $\mathrm { T V } ( P _ { + } , P _ { - } ) \leq 1 / 4$ , it would certify under the $\pi ^ { + }$ mixture with probability $> 1 / 2 - 1 / 4 \stackrel {  } { = } 1 / 4 > 1 / 8$ , a contradiction. Hence some feasible $\pi ^ { - }$ world is refused with probability $\geq 1 / 2 .$ . As $K _ { * } ( n , m )$ is finite at every $( n , m )$ and $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ admits arbitrarily fine finite weight oscillation (with weights confined to $\{ 1 / 2 , 3 / 2 \} )$ , a hard instance exists at every sample size, and the certifiable slack stays $\geq s _ { * }$ □

Remark 6 (What separates Model-B from Model-A, exactly). The barrier is the bilinear functional $\mathbb { E } _ { P } [ w S _ { \lambda } ( \eta - \alpha ) ]$ its sign is fixed by the alignment between the weight-signs u (seen only through the target covariate channel, which observes $Q _ { X } = w P _ { X } )$ and the loss-signs v (seen only through the source label channel). Neither marginal channel reveals the alignment $\sum _ { i } p _ { i } u _ { i } v _ { i }$ ; the two priors share both marginal laws and differ only in the sign-coupling, which is detectable only on cells occupied by both channels: a two-sample correlation-detection problem whose signal scales as the collision count, and which the adversary defeats with weight complexity K beyond any fixed $( n , m )$ . Model-A escapes because exact w reveals $u ,$ collapsing the risk test to the unbiased weighted labeled-source mean of Theorem 7; Model- ${ \bf - B ^ { \prime } }$ escapes because a pre-registered $K \cdot$ -cell partition caps the oscillation, so the per-split nuisance becomes estimable at the finite sample sizes of Theorem $^ { 3 , }$ The inconsistency is thus the information-theoretic shadow of the nuisance dimension that $\bar { \mathbf { B ^ { \prime } } }$ pays for explicitly; it does not assert a polynomial sample-complexity rate, only that no $( n , m )$ -uniform guarantee exists over the unrestricted class. This estimability boundary is the certification counterpart of the learnability/estimability lines for arbitrary covariate shift: reject-to-learn via a reliable-learner oracle [Kalai and Kanade, 2021] and structural recovery of $\mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ f ]$ from source labels and target covariates [Adil and Błasiok, 2026], with the pre-registered K-cell partition of Model-B<sup>′</sup> our analogue of the structure those works impose to regain tractability.

## B.5 Non-triviality remarks

Remark 7 $( \beta \to 0$ kills the impossibility; positivity persists) $\operatorname { A s } \beta \to 0 \colon ( \mathrm { i } )$ the above-frontier region $\{ \beta > \beta ^ { * } \}$ of Theorem 4 becomes empty; (ii) the n-axis window $c _ { 1 } \kappa ^ { - 1 } \sqrt { \beta / n } \to 0 ;$ (iii) the m-axis window $c _ { 2 } \sqrt { \beta / m }  0$ as well; both axes carry $\sqrt { \beta }$ and vanish with the floor. Meanwhile the class $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ and its overlap structure are unchanged: nothing about positivity moves. In Model-A the deep-safe block is then certifiable from labeled data alone at $n = \bar { O } ( \log ( 1 / \delta ) \bar { ) }$ with no dependence on the frontier slack; in Model-B a vestigial unlabeled requirement remains, confirming $\dot { C } _ { Q } ( \lambda ) > \dot { 0 }$ needs ${ \cal O } ( 1 \bar { 1 / C } _ { Q } ( \lambda ) )$ draws, but it is s-independent and does not scale with the frontier. The bite threshold is a property of $( \beta , \varphi )$ , not of overlap.

Remark 8 (Impossibility is loss-profile-driven, not support-driven). In $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ all target mass is identified (full overlap): the unidentified mass is zero in every construction, identically. Yet feasibility boundaries differ across worlds through $\varphi , { \bf a }$ functional of the loss profile η and $Q \colon$ the n-axis pair has identical $( { \dot { P _ { X } } } , Q _ { X } )$ (identical overlap geometry) and different feasibility verdicts at $( \alpha , \beta )$ . The impossibility here is therefore structurally disjoint from positivity results such as D’Amour et al. [2021].

Remark 9 (The floor is the m-axis; the model split is load-bearing). Remove the floor $( \beta = 0 ) \colon$ certification reduces to exhibiting λ with $R _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \leq o$ and $C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) > \mathsf { 0 }$ . In the m-axis pair, any threshold inside G has $R _ { \mathrm { Q } } = \alpha - a$ under every $Q \in { \bar { \mathcal { W } } } _ { B }$ sharing η (coverage changes; risk on G does not), so in Model-A (where $C _ { Q } ( \lambda ) \stackrel { \bullet } { = } \mathbb { E } _ { P } [ w \mathbf { 1 } _ { [ 0 , t ] } ]$ is computable from the known w), the deep-safe block is certifiable from labeled data alone with no dependence on s. The s-scaled m-axis hardness exists only because the floor forces acceptance up to the frontier. In Model-A the floor information moreover pools across channels (a labeled-sample route to $C _ { Q }$ opens), so the clean m-only floor axis is a Model-B phenomenon; a matching Model-A floor-axis lower bound would require a pooled-rate analysis that we do not claim; its constants are conjecture-grade and we leave it open.

## B.6 The overlap-created boundary, for contrast

For completeness we record what overlap-created impossibility looks like, outside the bounded-ratio class: it is governed by a sharp threshold in the unsupported target mass, with no role for sample sizes.

Proposition 2 (Sharp threshold under unsupported target mass). Drop the bounded-ratio assumption. Let $A \subseteq$ X be measurable with $P _ { X } ( A ) \ = \ 0$ and $Q _ { X } ( A ) \ = \ q _ { A } ,$ , and let the loss class be ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { A } ~ = ~ \{ \eta ~ : ~ \eta | _ { A ^ { c } } ~ = ~$ $\eta _ { 0 }$ (fixed, identifiable), $\eta | _ { A }$ arbitrary}. A procedure sees source labels $D _ { n } \sim P ^ { n }$ and unlabeled target covariates $U _ { m } \sim Q _ { X } ^ { m }$ , and outputs either a rule S with the certificate ${ } ^ { \mathfrak { c } \mathfrak { c } } R _ { Q } ( S ) \leq \alpha \wedge \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ S ] \geq \beta ^ { , , }$ or ⊥.

(a) (Lower bound.) $f q _ { A } > 1 - ( 1 - \alpha ) \beta$ , thenfor every procedure there is a member of $\mathcal { F } _ { A }$ on which, whenever the procedure certifies, the certificate is false; consequently any $( \alpha , \beta , \delta )$ -valid procedure certifies with probability at most δ there (it must refuse with probability $\geq 1 - \delta )$

(b) (Population/oracle achievability under a spare off-A budget.) Assume in addition that $Q | _ { A }$ is nonatomic, or that acceptance may be randomized on A, so that an accepted sub-mass of any size $\leq q _ { A }$ is attainable. Suppose there is an identifiable subset $S _ { 0 } \subseteq A ^ { c }$ with target mass $D _ { 0 } = \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ S _ { 0 } ]$ and (world-independent) off-A risk numerator $N _ { 0 } \doteq \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ \eta _ { 0 } S _ { 0 } ]$ , and set $a = \mathrm { m a x } \{ 0 , \beta - D _ { 0 } \} . \ I f a \leq q q _ { A }$ and $N _ { 0 } + a \le \alpha ( D _ { 0 } + a )$ then an explicit rule certifies validly over ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { A }$ . This is a population/oracle achievability statement: the rule uses the true masses $D _ { 0 } , N _ { 0 } , q _ { A }$ and an exactly sized accepted sliver ofA, so afinite-sample certificatefrom $( D _ { n } , U _ { m } )$ would additionally require these estimated with confidence margins and a strict (non-equality) spare-budget slack. (Without an atomless or randomized A the threshold can shift: an indivisible A-atom of mass > a cannot supply exactly $^ { a , }$ so the deterministic feasible set is smaller; the gap is the integrality gap of Appendix A.) In the risk-free special case $N _ { 0 } = 0 , D _ { 0 } = 1 - q _ { A }$ this condition reduces to $q _ { A } \leq 1 - ( 1 - \alpha ) \beta$ the exact complement ofthe lower bound (a); so $q _ { A } ^ { \star } = 1 - ( 1 - \alpha ) \beta$ is the tight threshold in that regime, while a positive off-A risk N tightens it.

Proof. Write $D = \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ S ]$ for accepted mass, $a _ { A } = \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ S \mathbf { 1 } _ { A } ]$ for accepted target mass in A, and $N _ { 0 } = \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ L S \mathbf { 1 } _ { A ^ { c } } ]$ for the off-A numerator, which is world-independent since $\eta _ { 0 }$ is fixed. Consider two worlds $\eta ^ { 0 } ( \eta \equiv 0 \mathrm { o n } A )$ and $\eta ^ { 1 } \left( \eta \equiv 1 \right)$ on $A )$ , both in ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { A }$ and identical off A.

Indistinguishability. Labels in $D _ { n }$ are observed only at the $X _ { i } ;$ since $P _ { X } ( A ) = 0$ , almost surely no $X _ { i } \in A$ , so no label from A is ever seen; $U _ { m }$ carries covariates only; the off-A law is shared. Hence the joint law of the observed data is identical under $\eta ^ { 0 }$ and $\eta ^ { 1 }$ , and any (possibly randomized) procedure emits the same certificate/rule law under both.

Ratio inflation. $R _ { Q } ^ { ( j ) } ( S ) = ( \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ L S \mathbf { 1 } _ { A ^ { c } } ] + \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ L S \mathbf { 1 } _ { A } ] ) / D ;$ the first term equals $N _ { 0 }$ in both worlds, while $\mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ L S \mathbf { 1 } _ { A } ] =$ $\mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ \eta S \mathbf { 1 } _ { A } ]$ equals 0 under $\eta ^ { 0 }$ and $a _ { A }$ under $\eta ^ { 1 }$ . Subtracting, $R _ { Q } ^ { 1 } - R _ { Q } ^ { 0 } = a _ { A } / D$

Sharp threshold. Suppose the procedure certifies with rule S (the same $S$ in both worlds, by indistinguishability) and the certificate is valid in both. Validity gives $D \geq \beta$ and $R _ { Q } ^ { 1 } ( S ) \leq \alpha$ . Since $\mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ S \mathbf { 1 } _ { A ^ { c } } ] \leq Q _ { X } ( A ^ { c } ) = \mathbf { \bar { 1 } } - q _ { A }$ , we have $a _ { A } = D - \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ S \mathbf { 1 } _ { A ^ { c } } ] \geq D - ( 1 - q _ { A } )$ , and with $N _ { 0 } \geq 0$

$$
R _ { Q } ^ { 1 } ( S ) = \frac { N _ { 0 } + a _ { A } } { D } \ \geq \ \frac { a _ { A } } { D } \ \geq \ 1 - \frac { 1 - q _ { A } } { D } .
$$

This lower bound is increasing in D, so it is minimized at the smallest feasible coverage $D ~ = ~ \beta \colon { \cal R } _ { \cal O } ^ { 1 } ( { \cal S } ) ~ \geq$ $1 - ( 1 - q _ { A } ) / \beta$ . Forced violation $R _ { Q } ^ { 1 } > \alpha$ holds whenever $1 - ( 1 - q _ { A } ) / \beta > \alpha ,$ i.e. exactly when $q _ { A } > 1 - ( 1 - \alpha ) \beta$ Thus above the threshold, every certifying procedure is false in world $\eta ^ { 1 } ;$ ; since {certify} ⊆ {invalid certificate} there, $( \alpha , \beta , \delta )$ -validity forces $\mathbb { P } ( \mathcal { A } \neq \bot ) \leq \dot { \delta }$ in world $\eta ^ { 1 }$

Achievability. Suppose the spare-budget condition of (b) holds: an identifiable $S _ { 0 } \subseteq A ^ { c }$ with $D _ { 0 } = \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ S _ { 0 } ] , N _ { 0 } =$ $\mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ \eta _ { 0 } S _ { 0 } ]$ , and $a = \operatorname* { m a x } \{ 0 , \beta - D _ { 0 } \} \leq q _ { A }$ satisfy $N _ { 0 } + a \le \alpha ( D _ { 0 } + a )$ . Take $S$ to accept $S _ { 0 }$ plus a sliver of A-mass a (available since $a \leq q _ { A } = Q _ { X } ( A )$ ; we assume $Q | _ { A }$ is nonatomic, or equivalently allow randomized acceptance on $A ,$ so that an accepted sub-mass of exactly a exists). Then $D = D _ { 0 } + a \ge \beta$ (either $D _ { 0 } \geq \beta$ with $a = 0 .$ , or $a = \beta - D _ { 0 }$ and $D = \beta )$ , and the accepted-A risk numerator is $\mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ \eta S \mathbf { 1 } _ { A } ] \le a$ for every η|<sub>A</sub> (loss $\leq 1$ on accepted mass a); hence, uniformly over ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { A }$

$$
R _ { Q } ( S ) = \frac { N _ { 0 } + \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ \eta S \mathbf { 1 } _ { A } ] } { D } ~ \le ~ \frac { N _ { 0 } + a } { D _ { 0 } + a } ~ \le ~ \alpha ,
$$

the last step by the spare-budget hypothesis. This is the correct worst case: the original $^ { * } N _ { 0 } = 0 ^ { , }$ worst case is valid only when η carries no risk on the accepted A<sup>c</sup>-mass; in general the off-A numerator $N _ { 0 }$ adds to the ratio $( \mathrm { e . g } ^ { } )$ $\alpha = \beta = \dot { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } , q _ { A } = 0 . 6 < 0 . 7 5$ with $\begin{array} { r } { \eta _ { 0 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \end{array}$ on A<sup>c</sup> forces $N _ { 0 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } D _ { 0 }$ and $R _ { Q }  \geq 0 . 6  > \alpha$ for the unconditional rule), which is exactly what the condition $N _ { 0 } + a ^ { - } \le \alpha ( D _ { 0 } + a )$ guards against. In the risk-free regime $N _ { 0 } = 0 , D _ { 0 } = 1 - q _ { A } ;$ when $q _ { A } > 1 - \beta$ one has $a = \beta - D _ { 0 } > 0 , D = \beta ,$ , and $R _ { Q } = a / \beta = 1 - ( 1 - q _ { A } ) \bar { / } \beta ;$ when $q _ { A } \le 1 - \beta$ one has $a = 0 , D = D _ { 0 }$ , and $R _ { Q } = 0$ . Compactly $R _ { Q } = a / ( D _ { 0 } + a ) = \operatorname* { m a x } \{ 0 , 1 - ( 1 - q _ { A } ) / \beta \} \leq$ α exactly when $q _ { A } \leq 1 - ( 1 - \alpha ) \beta$ , recovering the tight threshold of (a). A finite-sample version with $P _ { X } ( { \dot { A } } ) > 0$ but $n P _ { X } ( A ) \ll 1$ holds up to a $1 - n P _ { X } ( A )$ event, by the same argument conditioned on no source point landing in A. □

The contrast with Theorem 5 is the point: Proposition $2 \mathrm { { : } }$ impossibility is created by unsupported mass and is insensitive to $( n , m )$ , while the law’s impossibility lives entirely inside the full-overlap class, scales with $( n , m )$ , and vanishes as $\beta  0$ . Within $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ the overlap mechanism cannot occur; the floor mechanism is what remains, and it is the subject of this paper.

Its empirical-Bernstein range constant is the realized $B _ { \mathrm { e f f } } : = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { k } w _ { k }$ of the world rather than the declared bound $B ;$ this is Model-A information, since w is supplied exactly, and it only shrinks the range term.

The witness argument unions the same four tails, now at $\delta ^ { \prime \prime }$ , for a total of $4 \delta ^ { \prime \prime } = 2 \delta / | \Lambda | \leq \delta$ whenever $| \Lambda | \geq 2$ (both evaluated lattices have $| \Lambda | \in \{ 5 0 , 6 4 \} \rangle$ ). Neither variant dominates the other. The kernel’s per-bound level is looser than the displayed one $( \log ( 2 / \delta ^ { \prime \prime } ) =$ $\log ( 4 | \Lambda | / \delta )$ against $\log ( 8 | \Lambda | / \delta )$ , that is log 4,000 against log 8,000 at the audited $\delta = 0 . 0 5 , \mathsf { \bar { | } \Lambda | } = 5 0 )$ , which makes it certify more often; the displayed procedure carries the extra n-route floor, which the kernel lacks. We therefore claim no pointwise domination in either direction, and we report the oracle numbers as records of the two-family kernel rather than of the displayed procedure. Both are $( \alpha , \beta , \delta )$ )-valid, so the 0-violation tallies are statements about the arm that was run; the certification counts, onsets, and the oracle-versus-B<sup>′</sup> premium are likewise properties of this kernel.

(When instead $N _ { \mathrm { r i s k } } > N _ { \mathrm { f l o o r } }$ , the n-route clears the floor already within the risk requirement, so the region is the half-strip $\{ n \gtrsim N _ { \mathrm { r i s k } } \}$ alone and the m-route quadrant is redundant; the Model-A enlargement is then total.) Remark 11 (The variance functional is localized on both sides). On the lower-bound side, the adversary’s KL cost per unit of frontier flip is $\asymp ( \kappa s ) ^ { 2 } / ( \rho \bar { w } _ { U } ^ { 2 } \cdot \cdot \cdot )$ minimized over slices U inside the accepted region, so the binding quantity is an accepted-region weighted mass $( \rho \le \beta$ , weights w $| \mathbf { \sigma } _ { U } \rangle \rangle$ a shifted variant places the slice where w is large, and the channel scales like localized second moments of $\mathbb { E } _ { P } [ w ^ { \mathrm { i } } \dot { S } ]$ -type. On the upper-bound side, the empirical-Bernstein variance in Theorem 7 is $\mathrm { V a r } _ { P } ( w S _ { \lambda } ( L - \alpha ) ) \le \mathbb { E } _ { P } [ \stackrel { . } { w } ^ { 2 } S _ { \lambda } ]$ , the same localized family. Global $\mathbb { E } _ { P } [ \dot { w } ^ { 2 } ]$ (global ESS) appears on neither side. Exact constant matching between the two sides is open: the lower-bound constant involves the slice geometry, the upper the full accepted-set variance; we flag this and do not claim it.

Remark 12 (Relation to the floor-free minimax bound). The matching single-axis lower bound for conformal risk control under non-monotone losses [Aldirawi et al., 2026] concerns excess risk when selecting among a finite grid of tuning-parameter values, with no coverage constraint: one resource axis, no coverage-floor parameter. Our lower bound differs in (i) the m-axis existing at all (it is the floor’s shadow, Remark 9), (ii) β appearing inside the n-axis constant through the inframarginal mass, and (iii) the target being a certification (decide-or-refuse) task. The techniques are neighbors in the same minimax toolkit [Tsybakov, 2009], theirs a Fano argument over the tuning-parameter grid and ours Le Cam two-point pairs, one per axis, but neither statement implies the other.

Summary of the three claims. Table 3 (§4, beside the map) states, for each claim of Theorem 1, its information model, the assumptions it adds beyond the standing ones, what it proves, and the gap it leaves open. This makes the cross-model (not within-model) structure of the match scannable.

Assembly: proof of Theorem 1. Claim 1 is Theorem 5 with the additive single-family combination of Appendix B.3. Claim 2 is Theorem 7: in the worst case over the class, the $\mathrm { L R } _ { \mathrm { m a r g i n } }$ witness has $\bar { V } _ { \lambda _ { s } } \~ \dot { \leq } \mathbb { E } _ { P } [ w ^ { 2 } S _ { \lambda _ { s } } ] \leq B \hat { \mathbb { E } } _ { P } ^ { \mathrm { ~ ~ } } [ w S _ { \lambda _ { s } } ] =$ $B C _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { s } ) \ : \le \ : B ( \beta + s ) \asymp B \beta$ in the local regime $s \leq c _ { 0 } \beta ;$ the empirical-Bernstein range term is dominated via the witness margin $\kappa s / 8 \leq - G _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { s } ) \leq C _ { Q } ( \lambda _ { s } ) \leq ( 1 + c _ { 0 } ) \beta ,$ , giving $B / ( \kappa s ) \leq 8 ( 1 + c _ { 0 } ) B \beta / ( \kappa s ) ^ { 2 }$ , so with B treated as a constant the displayed conditions reduce to n $\iota \gtrsim \beta \log ( | \Lambda | / \delta ) / ( \kappa s ) ^ { 2 }$ and m $\gtrsim \beta \log ( | \Lambda | / \delta ) / s ^ { 2 }$ (the oracle Model-A upper attains the same two-axis rates as the Model-B lower bound, up to constants and logarithms, while no distribution-free unknown-weight matching upper is claimed), and indeed none can attain the Model-A rate over $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ (Theorem 6); the formal statement’s pooled floor option (the n-route) only weakens the requirement. Claim 3 is Theorems 2–3 with Proposition 1 (proofs in Appendix F); the geometry sentence is Remark 11; the phase-diagram sentence is Corollary 1 (proved in this appendix from Theorem $7 \mathrm { { s } }$ disjunctive sample condition and Corollaries $3 { - } 4 ) ;$ the within-Model- ${ \bf \cdot B } ^ { \dagger }$ two-resource-axis match asserted in the headline is Corollary $\bar { 2 ; }$ and the nuisance-necessity clause is Theorem 8 (Appendix H): the K-free core necessary (lattice-valued) in the known-η submodel, the histogram $B ^ { 2 } K$ split not proved necessary, unknown-η open. □

Why no matching Model-B upper exists. In Model-B the floor side is estimable label-free but the risk side is not: $G _ { Q }$ depends on w, unidentified beyond $w \le B \left( \ S 3 . 3 \right)$ . The routes one might try either import estimable structure $( \mathbf { B } ^ { \prime \prime } \mathbf { s }$ pre-registered partition, at Proposition 1’s nuisance price) or guard against the worst weight in the box, and Proposition 4 exhibits an instance where that envelope certifies no population-feasible point (refusing with probability $\geq \dot { 1 } - | \Lambda | \delta _ { f } ^ { \prime } )$ while $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ certifies at finite samples. This is not an accident of those two routes: Theorem 6 shows that no Model-B procedure attains the Model-A guarantee over $\mathcal { W } _ { B } \colon$ over the full unknown-weight class the problem is not consistently solvable at any $( n , m )$ , because certifying the risk requires detecting the alignment between weight-signs (seen only through target covariates) and loss-signs (seen only through source labels), a two-sample correlation-detection task the adversary defeats with weight complexity beyond any fixed sample size (Remark 6). The cross-model gap is therefore fundamental, and the $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ complexity restriction is necessary for consistency, not a convenience. Positive certification thus requires either oracle weights (Model-A) or an explicit shift-complexity restriction $( \mathrm { M o d e l { - } B ^ { \prime } { ' } s }$ pre-registered finite K): the inconsistency is a statement about the unrestricted bounded-ratio class; it does not assert that a fixed-complexity unknown-weight model is inconsistent, and $\mathbf { B } ^ { \prime }$ is exactly such a fixed-complexity restriction, certifying at finite samples at its nuisance price.

## D Relaxed Linear Crossings versus Discrete-Lattice Plateaus

The exponent −2 in the bite divergence is tied to the linear budget law (2), which is generic (Lemma 1). Under bounded loss the relaxed budget in fact admits no other nondegenerate exponent: every nondegenerate frontier crossing is asymptotically linear, the only alternative being a degenerate zero-local-budget tie (Proposition 3). The flat “plateau” behavior an early coarse-lattice pilot suggested is therefore not a property of the relaxed budget (a $\gamma = \infty$ relaxed exponent is a category error for a concave Lipschitz budget) but of a fixedfinite lattice, which we record separately as a constant-budget lower-bound lemma (Lemma 3). We then explain the pilot’s intermediate fitted slope as a mixture of the two.

Proposition 3 (Relaxed crossings are linear: a dichotomy). Let $\varphi$ be the relaxed budget of a world in $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ with $\alpha \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ , and assume a crossingfrontier: $0 < \beta ^ { * } \leq 1$ with $\varphi ( { \boldsymbol { \beta } } ^ { * } ) = 0$ (the regular-margin setting; the non-crossing case $\bar { \beta } ^ { \ast } \bar { = } 1 , \varphi ( 1 ) > 0$ and the empty-frontier case $\beta ^ { * } = 0$ are excluded and treated as boundary cases below). Since $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } \in [ 0 , 1 ] , \varphi$ is concave and 1-Lipschitz on $[ 0 , \beta ^ { * } ]$ (its a.e. derivative $\alpha - \eta _ { Q } ^ { * } \in [ - 1 , 1 ] )$ with $\varphi \geq 0$ there. Exactly one ofthefollowing holds as s ↓ 0:

(i) (nondegenerate crossing) $\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ) > 0$ for some $s \in ( 0 , \beta ^ { * } ] _ { : }$ ; then $\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ) = \kappa _ { * } s + o ( s )$ with $\kappa _ { * } =$ $- \varphi _ { - } ^ { \prime } ( \breve { \beta } ^ { * } ) \in ( 0 , 1 ]$ . A regular margin (κ, s<sub>0</sub>), $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } \ge \alpha + \kappa$ a.e. on $[ \beta ^ { * } - s _ { 0 } , \beta ^ { * } ]$ , then holds for every fixed $\kappa \in ( 0 , \kappa _ { * } )$ once s<sub>0</sub> is small enough (in general only $\kappa \leq \kappa _ { * }$ , with the attainable $\kappa \uparrow \kappa _ { \ast }$ as $s _ { 0 } \downarrow 0 ;$ the lower-bound constant κ need not equal the limiting slope $\kappa _ { * } ) .$ . The labeled-sample scale $n \gtrsim \beta / ( \kappa s ) ^ { 2 }$ in this regime is not a corollary of the dichotomy but Theorem 5’s two-point bound under its own hypotheses (Remark 13): a necessary conditionfor that construction, not a matching $\asymp ;$

(ii) (degenerate tie) $\varphi \equiv 0$ on $[ 0 , \beta ^ { * } ]$ , i.e. $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * } = \alpha$ a.e. there (the zero-local-budget case, left open).

No relaxed exponent $\gamma \neq 1$ arises: $\gamma < 1$ would force $\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ) / s \to \infty ,$ , violating Lipschitzness, while $\gamma > 1$ (a fortiori $\gamma = \infty )$ would force $\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ) = o ( s )$ , i.e. case (ii).

Proof. Set $f ( s ) : = \varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s )$ for $s \in [ 0 , \beta ^ { * } ] \colon f$ is concave, 1-Lipschitz, $f ( 0 ) = 0 ;$ , and $f \geq 0$ . By concavity and $f ( 0 ) = 0$ , for $0 < s < t \leq \beta ^ { * }$ we have $\begin{array} { r } { f ( s ) \geq \frac { s } { t } f ( t ) } \end{array}$ , so $s \mapsto f ( s ) / s$ is nonincreasing on $( 0 , \beta ^ { * } ] ;$ ; the 1-Lipschitz bound gives $0 \leq f ( s ) / s \leq 1$ . Hence $\kappa _ { * } : = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { s \downarrow 0 } \stackrel { \cdot } { f } ( s ) / s = f _ { + } ^ { \prime } ( 0 )$ exists in [0, 1], with $\kappa _ { * } = - \varphi _ { - } ^ { \prime } ( \beta ^ { * } )$ . If $f ( t ) > 0$ for some $t \in ( 0 , \beta ^ { * } ]$ , then $\kappa _ { * } \geq f ( t ) / t > 0$ , giving (i) with $f ( \dot { s } ) = \kappa _ { * } s + o ( s )$ . For the regular-margin claim of (i), write $\begin{array} { r } { f ( s ) = \int _ { 0 } ^ { s } ( \eta _ { Q } ^ { \ast } ( \beta ^ { \ast } - v ) - \alpha ) } \end{array}$ dv (since $f ^ { \prime } ( v ) = \eta _ { Q } ^ { * } ( \beta ^ { * } - v ) - \alpha \mathrm { a . e }$ . and $f ( 0 ) = 0 )$ ; the integrand is nonincreasing in v (the rearrangement $\eta _ { Q } ^ { * }$ is nondecreasing) and tends to κ<sub>∗</sub> as v $\downarrow 0 .$ , so for every fixed $\kappa < \kappa _ { : }$ <sub>∗</sub> it is $\geq \kappa \mathrm { a . e } .$ . on $( 0 , s _ { 0 } )$ for $s _ { 0 }$ small enough, a regular margin $( \kappa , s _ { 0 } )$ , while any regular-margin κ gives $f ( s ) / s \geq \kappa ,$ , hence $\kappa \leq \kappa _ { * }$ . Otherwise $f ( t ) = 0$ for all small $t > 0 ;$ a nonnegative concave f with $f ( 0 ) = 0$ that vanishes on an interval at the origin vanishes on all of $[ 0 , \beta ^ { * } ]$ (else concavity $f ( \lambda \mathbf { \bar { } } u ) \geq \lambda f ( u )$ is violated), so $f \equiv 0 , \mathrm { i . e . } \eta _ { Q } ^ { * } = \alpha \mathrm { a . e }$ . on $[ 0 , \beta ^ { * } ]$ , which is (ii). The cases are exhaustive and mutually exclusive. The exponent claim follows since $f ( s ) \asymp s ^ { \gamma }$ forces $f ( s ) / s \to \infty ( \gamma < 1 )$ or $f ( s ) / s \to 0 ( \gamma > 1 )$ , the latter making the nonincreasing ratio identically 0, i.e. case (ii). □

Remark 13 (The labeled-axis rate is imported from Theorem 5, not the dichotomy). Proposition 3(i) fixes only the geometry of the relaxed budget, $\varphi ( \beta ^ { * } - s ) = \kappa _ { * } s + o ( s )$ The labeled-sample scale $n \stackrel { . } { \sim } \beta / ( \kappa s ) ^ { \dot { 2 } }$ is the separate two-point $\left( \mathrm { L e } \mathrm { C a m } \right)$ lower bound of Theorem 5 (Appendix B.1) and inherits all of its hypotheses: a world in $\mathcal { W } _ { B }$ with regular margin $( \kappa , s _ { 0 } )$ , the local regime $s \in ( 0 , s _ { 0 } \wedge c _ { 0 } \beta ]$ , valid coverage $\beta + s \le 1$ , confidence $\delta \leq 1 / 8$ , and the construction’s interior Bernoulli levels in [0.1, 0.95]. It is a necessary condition for that construction family, not a matching $\asymp ,$ and does not follow from concavity or the regular margin alone; at a boundary Bernoulli level the per-sample KL can be linear, not quadratic, in the perturbation.

A lower bound on the required sample size that does not carry the linear-crossing $s ^ { - 2 }$ factor is thus a phenomenon of coarse-lattice certification (where, between adjacent lattice coverages, the operative budget does not shrink with the within-cell floor position; Remark 4) rather than of the relaxed budget; we record a per-floor lower bound exhibiting it directly.

Lemma 3 (Finite-lattice constant-budget plateau). F $\ i x \alpha \in [ 0 . 2 , 0 . 9 ] , \delta \leq 1 / 8 ,$ and a constant $\varphi _ { 0 } > 0 .$ . Fix afloor cell $[ \beta _ { - } , \beta _ { + } ] \subseteq ( 0 , 0 . 6 ]$ with $\varphi _ { 0 } \leq \textstyle \frac { 1 } { 8 } \beta _ { - } \operatorname* { m i n } \{ \alpha , 1 - \alpha \}$ . For eachfloor $\mathsf { \bar { \beta } } \in [ \beta _ { - } , \beta _ { + } ]$ there are worlds $W _ { 1 , \beta } , W _ { 2 , \beta } \in \mathcal { W } _ { B }$ and a finite nested-threshold lattice containing the coverage-β threshold, whose accepted block carries risk budget φ<sub>0</sub>, on which no $( \alpha , \beta , \delta )$ -valid lattice procedure certifies thefeasible world $W _ { 1 , \beta }$ with probability $\geq 1 / 2$ once n $\stackrel { \cdot } { \sim } \beta / \stackrel { \cdot } { \varphi _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } } .$ This per-β two-point lower bound is $\Omega ( \beta / \varphi _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } )$ and contains no $s ^ { - 2 }$ factor: holding the accepted block’s budgetfixed at φ (instead ofletting it shrink to κs as on a refining grid) removes the $s ^ { - 2 }$ growth ofthe linear-crossing scale of Proposition 3(i). The relaxed slack ofeach construction is itselfbounded below by a constant $( \beta ^ { * } = 3 \beta / 2$ below, so $s = \beta / 2 ) ,$ , so the absence ofan $s ^ { - 2 }$ factor is genuine, not a vanishing-s artifact. (We do not claim a singlefixed world or one lattice point serving the whole cell $[ \beta _ { - } , \beta _ { + } ] ,$ ; each β uses its own $W _ { 1 , \beta }$ and coverage-β threshold.)

Proof. Fix $\beta \in [ \beta _ { - } , \beta _ { + } ]$ and instantiate the n-axis pair of Appendix B.1 with the constant budget $\varphi _ { 0 }$ in place of κs. Take $\mathcal { X } = [ 0 , 1 ] , P _ { X } \overset { \cdot } { = } Q _ { X } \overset { \cdot } { = } \mathrm { U n i f } [ 0 , 1 ]$ (so $w \equiv 1 )$ and thresholds accepting [0, t]; both worlds have Bernoulli conditional losses $L \mid X \sim \operatorname { B e r n } ( \eta _ { j } ( X ) )$ and share $( P _ { X } , Q _ { X } )$ .
